#3037. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3036. Meet the Only "Magnificent Seven" Stock That's Outperforming the S&P 500 and Nasdaq This Year (Hint: It's Not Nvidia)

Apple is hovering around an all-time high despite a broader sell-off in tech stocks.

The "Magnificent Seven" was popularized in 2023 by Bank of America analyst Michael Hartnett as a term for seven rapidly appreciating tech-focused growth companies. In 2023, Apple (AAPL -1.90%), Nvidia (NVDA +2.29%), Microsoft (MSFT +2.73%), Alphabet (GOOG +0.08%) (GOOGL +0.37%), Amazon (AMZN +2.06%), Meta Platforms (META +2.36%), and Tesla (TSLA -1.32%) were instrumental in driving the Nasdaq Composite's (^IXIC +0.83%) monster 43.4% gain -- with all seven companies posting even bigger returns.

Big gains followed in 2024, with every Magnificent Seven stock except for Microsoft outperforming the Nasdaq and S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.36%). But in 2025, only Nvidia and Alphabet outperformed both indexes. And after last week's sell-off, the only Magnificent Seven stock left standing above the indexes is Apple, which surpassed Nvidia as the world's most valuable company on Oct. 30.

Here's what separates Apple from its Magnificent Seven stock peers, and if the tech stock is a buy now.

Apple is up 26.8% year-to-date, outpacing the major indexes, the broader tech sector, and its Magnificent Seven peers.

Apple is unique because artificial intelligence (AI) capital expenditures (capex) don't make or break its investment thesis. By comparison, Amazon, Alphabet, Microsoft, Meta Platforms, and Tesla continue to ramp up their AI spending. And although Nvidia has low capex, its major customers are the hyperscalers, so it needs them to see a return on their chip and associated AI infrastructure investments to justify future spending.

AAPL Free Cash Flow (Quarterly) data by YCharts

At the beginning of the AI ramp-up, cash-cow companies like Amazon and Alphabet were able to support higher spending with free cash flow (FCF) alone. But spending has grown so massive that Amazon, Alphabet, and Meta Platforms are now FCF-negative.

Alphabet is taking on debt, diluting shareholders by issuing stock, and is guiding for even higher spending in 2027. Investors are losing patience, with Alphabet experiencing a record $505 billion market cap plunge in a single session and plummeting around a 52-week low. Apple is the exact opposite narrative.

Apple's balance sheet is in impeccable shape. It continues to generate gobs of FCF that it can use to reinvest in the business or return to shareholders through buybacks and dividends.

Apple isn't spending record capex to build data centers. Rather, its focus is on providing products that allow consumers and enterprises to interact with AI, such as the first touchscreen MacBook Pro, its first foldable iPhone Duo, its smart home system HomeView, smart glasses, an AI-powered pendant, and more. Apple is also directly passing along higher memory chip costs to customers through price increases. To help bridge the affordability gap, it is offering a new leasing program facilitated by Klarna, in addition to the multi-year service contracts already offered by carriers.

Apple doesn't need to spend a lot on AI capex because new product updates and AI features on its devices are manageable costs. Developers depend on Apple App Store downloads to gain traction, all the while funneling more revenue to Apple in fees.

What makes Apple particularly appealing is that it is exposed to virtually all of the benefits of AI adoption with very few of the cons. Whereas frontier models like Anthropic's Claude, OpenAI's ChatGPT, and Alphabet's Gemini are all competing for user engagement. Amazon, Microsoft, and Alphabet are racing to attract cloud spend. By comparison, Apple is a catch-all way to bet on AI innovation and productivity improvements.

Outside of AI, Apple has evolved into a modern-day consumer staples company more than a tech stock. Its integrated ecosystem of products and services is essential for many users. And demand has proven incredibly sticky despite inflationary and cost-of-living pressures.

Apple is hovering around an all-time high because AI acts as a cherry on top of its investment thesis rather than the entire sundae. If there were a cyclical slowdown in AI spending or an AI-driven stock market correction, Apple would be one of the best-positioned tech stocks to weather it.

The problem with buying the stock now is that Apple trades at 39.5 times earnings and 36.1 times forward earnings -- which is a significant premium compared to its 10-year median price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 27.6. However, Apple's valuation is digestible for investors who truly view Apple as a recession-proof, consumer staples/tech hybrid. Especially when compared to recession-resistant companies like Walmart (WMT -2.22%) and Costco Wholesale (COST -2.05%), which are even more expensive than Apple. Walmart fetches a 42.8 forward P/E, and Costco's is 44.4.

Investors willing to pay a premium price for a safe stock may want to consider buying Apple, even around an all-time high. But investors who believe that AI spending will eventually pay off for hyperscalers will find stocks like Alphabet and Amazon at far more attractive valuations.

Bank of America is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Daniel Foelber has positions in Nvidia. #3030. Cerebras Systems vs. Innodata: Which Tech Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

One of these AI stocks is growing fast but burning cash, while the other is already profitable and cheaper on sales and forward earnings.

The artificial intelligence (AI) boom is shifting from experimental software to massive infrastructure builds. Choosing between hardware pioneer Cerebras Systems (CBRS +1.51%) and data specialist Innodata (INOD +1.40%) depends on your appetite for risk.

Cerebras Systems manufactures giant chips designed to process AI workloads faster than traditional hardware. Meanwhile, Innodata focuses on the fuel for those chips, providing the high-quality data engineering required to train large language models. Both companies play critical roles in the expanding AI ecosystem, but their financial profiles and market positions differ significantly.

Cerebras Systems operates in the competitive semiconductor landscape, where innovation, speed, and efficiency are crucial. The company creates massive wafer-scale engines, which are essentially dinner-plate-sized chips that integrate compute and memory to reduce latency. This specialized hardware supports both AI training and inference, offering these capabilities through physical data centers and flexible cloud services to customers worldwide. Its mission is to accelerate the development of artificial intelligence by providing a faster, more efficient alternative to standard industry processors.

On a trailing-12-month (TTM) basis through the June quarter, revenue reached nearly $681 million, representing a substantial 91% increase over the previous year. The company reported a net loss of $512 million during the same period.

Based on its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 5.8x, indicating the company's ability to cover short-term debts with short-term assets. It carries a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.2x, indicating a small amount of debt relative to shareholders' equity.

TTM free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was negative $680 million. This negative cash flow indicates the company is spending more on capital investments than it is generating from its core operations.

Innodata specializes in data engineering and evaluation frameworks that help major technology firms build generative AI systems. The company operates under master service agreements and counts five of the "Magnificent Seven" firms as major clients. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, as one specific customer accounted for nearly 58% of total revenue in 2025. Its strategy focuses on providing the human expertise and data labeling necessary to refine complex AI models.

TTM revenue reached $317 million as of the June quarter, representing a year-over-year increase of 39%. The company reported net income of roughly $47 million for the period, maintaining positive earnings for two consecutive years. Innodata has remained profitable over the last two fiscal years.

As for financial health, the June 2026 balance sheet shows a current ratio of nearly 1.7x, indicating strong short-term liquidity. The debt-to-equity ratio, which measures total debt relative to shareholders' equity, is nearly 0.1x.

TTM free cash flow was approximately $184 million, providing cash for potential reinvestment.

Cerebras Systems faces intense competition in the specialized hardware market, particularly from much larger incumbents with vast research budgets. The company must continually innovate to prove its wafer-scale architecture remains superior to more conventional networking and processing solutions.

Innodata contends with massive customer concentration, making it vulnerable to the budget shifts of just one or two major technology partners. Both Cerebras and Innodata face the same risk in terms of a broad slowdown in AI spending. Any slowdown in global AI infrastructure spending could impact their growth trajectory.

Innodata looks cheaper based on its P/S ratio, measuring sales over the past twelve months, and its Forward P/E relative to future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

I would buy Innodata mainly because it is generating robust revenue growth and free cash flow, while Cerebras is reporting large losses on the bottom line. Analysts expect Innodata's earnings to grow at an annualized rate of 22% in the coming years. That can support its forward P/E.

Innodata's revenue surged 58% year over year in the second quarter, with adjusted operating profit up 92%. It is positioning itself as an AI deployment assurance layer to help enterprises safely use agents. It recently launched an AI Cyber Training Suite to train coding agents and fix issues in a company's software.

I would wait to buy Cerebras until it shows a clearer path to sustainable cash flow growth. It has significant growth potential, with highly efficient, fast computing systems that could compete with traditional GPU-powered systems. Still, the stock is very expensive for a business that has not yet generated consistent profits.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Innodata. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3027. Why Altria Group Shares Bumped 15.9% Higher In October

Investors want safer stocks amid market volatility.

Shares of Altria Group (MO -0.30%) jumped 15.9% last month, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence. The tobacco giant is not the most exciting stock, but it keeps delivering steady earnings for shareholders and is being prized as a safe haven amid market volatility. The company reported strong earnings last quarter, and investors were pleased with the results.

Here's why the stock was up in October, and whether shares are a buy right now.

Altria sells tobacco and nicotine products, primarily in the United States market. Last quarter, it delivered solid results despite the decline in cigarette usage in the country.

Its volumes for premium Marlboro cigarettes declined by 6.4% year-over-year, but growth in its discount category led to overall cigarette volume declines of 2.9%. Through price increases, Altria is able to offset these volume declines, with the smokeables business segment reporting 3.7% revenue growth net of excise taxes, and similar levels of earnings growth.

Its oral tobacco category had flat revenues, but its on! nicotine pouch brand saw 13.3% growth in the period, which investors were likely happy about as well. Management keeps repurchasing stock to lower shares outstanding, while the dividend was raised in August, marking 57 straight years of consecutive dividend hikes.

With Altria's share price zooming to new highs, it now trades at a price-to-earnings ratio (P/E) of 16. This is one of its highest levels in years, and indicates that investors want safer stocks, with volatility appearing in the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Altria should be able to keep up its steady earnings growth in the years ahead. It can raise prices to offset volume declines, while moving into new categories like nicotine pouches and electronic vapor that are experiencing user growth in the United States and around the globe. Through share repurchases, it can keep increasing earnings per share (EPS), which will fuel even more dividend hikes that can be paid to shareholders.

However, right now, a P/E ratio of 16 does not look overly appealing for a slow-growth stock. The dividend is currently yielding just under 6%, which is pretty good for an income stock, but don't expect much more in returns (dividends plus dividend growth) in the coming years because of volume declines, an inherent headwind for the business.

#3025. Why Cipher Digital Stock Lost 53.9% in October

Is Cipher Digital’s October crash a buying opportunity? The answer depends on the debt market.

October was a rough month to own Cipher Digital (CIFR +18.37%). According to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence, the stock fell 53.9%, while the S&P 500 barely blinked with a 0.9% dip.

The data center developer was already sliding alongside other AI infrastructure stocks as investors grew nervous about the sector's heavy borrowing needs. Cipher stood out by raising $850 million of convertible debt on Oct. 13.

On paper, the deal sounds almost too good. Cipher sold convertible notes due 2032 with a 0% coupon, so it pays no interest for six years. Noteholders can swap their bonds into stock at $15.31 per share, and Cipher spent about $90 million on a capped call to blunt dilution. It also retired about $166 million of older 2030 notes in exchange for fresh shares.

Investors focused on dilution and timing. The stock fell 12.8% on Oct. 13 and another 12.4% the next day as the bond deal priced.

Part of the proceeds funded a $359 million investment in Cipher's Barber Lake campus, where tenant change orders and rising labor costs had pushed construction expenses above earlier guidance.

Then came a double whammy on Oct. 30. A Barclays analyst warned that AI financing problems were already here, and a record $6.3 billion Binance hack knocked crypto stocks around. Don't forget that Cipher used to focus on Bitcoin (BTC +1.15%) mining rather than AI computing, and that 78% of Q3 revenues still came from Bitcoin mining. So the stock caught both punches on the chin and fell another 10%.

Here's the odd part. Cipher has $16.6 billion in contracted revenue, with leases averaging 17.3 years. Management expects roughly $864 million of annual net operating income once its three contracted campuses stabilize. At the end of October, the whole company was worth about $3 billion. CEO Tyler Page described these sites as AI expressways that will collect tolls for decades.

But Cipher owes about $6.7 billion, its next projects need more capital, and the credit market isn't feeling generous. Construction costs remain under pressure, and nearly all of its sites sit in Texas, where data center regulation faces growing scrutiny. Those concerns popped up again in early November, when the stock showed extreme volatility around Cipher's mixed third-quarter report and the midterm elections.

One strange silver lining: with shares under $8, that $15.31 conversion price looks a long way off. Dilution from the new notes isn't an urgent problem at today's prices, though a lot can happen before 2032.

For long-term investors, the key question is whether Cipher can sign new leases on terms that justify its heavy borrowing. Its 5.9-gigawatt development pipeline offers plenty of opportunities. The financing environment will limit how many it can pursue.

Anders Bylund has positions in Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Bitcoin. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3022. Pfizer: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The patent-expiry question is still open: whether cutting costs now buys enough time for late-stage trials to replace fading blockbuster revenue.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Pfizer (PFE -2.19%) raised its full-year revenue outlook to a range of $62 billion to $64 billion. The company faces upcoming loss of exclusivity, or patent expirations, on key commercial drugs later this decade. Whether it can replace that fading revenue comes down to reinvesting cash into pipeline development and cost efficiency. Three key takeaways from the call stand out.

New treatments and recent acquisitions are generating immediate sales growth that buffers against legacy product declines. That top-line momentum gives management the leeway to fund new clinical programs while the transition plays out.

Manufacturing cuts and operational efficiency are shielding operating margins as older blockbusters lose patent protection. Management expects these structural cost reductions to fund heavy research and development investments on the other side.

Long-term value creation depends on expanding oncology pipelines and launching monthly obesity treatments. Once the patent transition finishes, management projects a sustainable compound annual growth rate, or CAGR, on the other side.

Clinical catalysts and regulatory decisions are approaching rapidly across core oncology and immunology focus areas. And management isn't hiding its drive to execute. Deep commercial reach in cancer care and an expanding pipeline of monthly weight-loss peptides position the business to rebuild its product portfolio. Management is watching clinical milestones and pricing discipline across international markets.

With cost-cutting programs protecting margins through the patent cliff, the long-term thesis comes down to converting late-stage trials into commercial revenue.

#3018. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#2999. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2996. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#2995. Adetomiwa Adebawore signs $35 million Colts extension one week before trade deadline

The Colts have been at the center of a lot of trade deadline rumors — and moves — ahead of Nov. 10.

But one player is staying in Indianapolis a little longer.

Defensive lineman Adetomiwa Adebawore has agreed to a two-year, $35 million extension to stay with the team through the 2028 season, NFL Network reported Tuesday.

Adebawore, 25, had been slated to hit free agency in March.

The deal, according to NFL Network, includes a $13 million signing bonus.

Indianapolis Colts defensive tackle Adetomiwa Adebawore reacts after a sack against the Los Angeles Rams at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, Calif., Sept. 28, 2025. MediaNews Group via Getty Images

In what has been a trying year for the Colts, Adebawore is having what is projected to be a career season.

He's already matched a career high with eight sacks and has 19 tackles — four of which have been for a loss.

Adebawore ranks 12th out of 139 interior defensive linemen this season, according to Pro Football Focus' grading methodology.

The Northwestern product has racked up 10.5 sacks with 67 tackles across his first four seasons since getting selected in the sixth round of the 2023 draft.

October came with a lot of change in Indianapolis, to say the least. The team fired general manager Chris Ballard before trading guard Quenton Nelson to the Seahawks and defensive end DeForest Buckner to the 49ers.

Adetomiwa Adebawore of the Indianapolis Colts looks on prior to an NFL preseason football game against the New England Patriots in Foxborough, Mass., Aug. 13, 2026. Getty Images

Plenty of rumors and speculation have surrounded the remaining stars on the Colts, with most of the centering on running back Jonathan Taylor.

ESPN reported last weekend that the Colts are open to trading the three-time Pro Bowler — with The Post's Ryan Dunleavy confirming the Giants have interest.

Taylor has 610 rushing yards and eight touchdowns this year, and could be a big help to contending teams.

The Colts are sitting at 2-6, but their first-round pick belongs to the Jets via the Sauce Gardner trade last season.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

#2987. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2984. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2981. Josh Jacobs needs to display more (or any) contrition

In May 2026, Packers running back Josh Jacobs crossed a line that should never be crossed. And neither he nor his representatives are acting accordingly in what should be an effort to seek redemption.

He pleaded no contest to battery of a woman. He's fighting in court to keep the video secret. He's responding to additional allegations through a strident statement from his lawyers attacking the accusers.

The most tone-deaf gesture of all came last Thursday night, when he celebrated a touchdown by pretending to wear, and to be released from, handcuffs.

His implausible explanation, which accused Prime Video of not showing enough of the celebration and attacked the media for interpreting the images with their lyin' eyes, was contradicted by the full video of the event.

That's not the way to play this. His no-contest plea is equivalent to saying, "I did it." He should be contrite. He should be apologetic. He should be seeking redemption.

The Packers continue to stand behind him, primarily because he's the best running back on the roster and they want to win games. Their reported interest in Colts running back Jonathan Taylor strongly suggests that, if the Packers could get Taylor, the support for Jacobs would immediately evaporate.

Here's the basic reality. If the Packers were to cut Jacobs, would anyone sign him? The Chiefs took plenty of heat last week for adding kicker Justin Tucker to the practice squad; they may not be inclined to give another second chance to a player whose arrival would compound the negative reaction to Tucker's arrival.

The reality, in every sport at every level, is obvious. If the player has enough skill and ability, someone will find a way to justify behavior that shouldn't be tolerated by an industry that relies so heavily on support from the public, both directly through ticket sales and TV ratings and indirectly through taxpayer funding for stadium projects.

Still, Jacobs has done himself no favors by adopting a posture of defiance. He did something no man should ever do. He's lucky he's good enough at football to not lose his job over it.

If he was at the bottom of the depth chart in Green Bay, he would have been gone a long time ago.

#2977. Steph Curry’s historic streak ends at worst possible time in Warriors thriller

SAN FRANCISCO — Stephen Curry had stepped to the foul line 50 times this season with the same result. Swish. Bottom of the net. Put a point on the board.

So, when he was fouled with the Warriors leading by two and less than six seconds left Monday night against the Suns, the game had to be as good as over … right?

Only after Curry clanked a free throw for the first time this season. Dillon Brooks grabbed the rebound and passed it ahead to Collin Gillespie, who got off a last-second heave from 36 feet.

It was a clean look, albeit an unlikely one, and could have sent the game to overtime. The shot fell short and Curry's ill-timed miss proved insignificant.

Stephen Curry of the Golden State Warriors looks on during a game against the Phoenix Suns on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Chase Center in San Francisco. NBAE via Getty Images

But in a game that he finished with 34 total — his third in a row with 30 or more — it was the one point Curry left on the board that drew the attention.

"I mean, he's just made so many big free throws in his career," coach Steve Kerr said. "Obviously he makes all of those. The law of averages catches up every once in a while."

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry (30) shoots against the Phoenix Suns in an NBA basketball game in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

In addition to being the greatest 3-point shooter in history, it would make sense then that Curry keeps the same standard in a controlled setting, 8 feet, 9 inches closer to the basket. His 91.3% career rate makes him the most efficient foul shooter of all time.

Curry is also responsible for two of the NBA's four longest stretches of success from the stripe to begin a season. With his streak, he was closing in on himself (52 in a row in 2017-18) for third place and Steve Nash (53 in 2007-08) for second for the most foul shots without a miss.

It would have taken some time to snatch the record away from Jose Calderon (84 in 2008-09).

But with a chance to put the game on ice and move into a tie for third place, for the first time in a competitive game in 204 days, Curry's shot clanked off the rim, into the hands of Brooks.

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry shoots against Phoenix Suns guard Devin Booker and center Khaman Maluach in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

Fortunately for the Warriors, the 11 free throws, five 3s and four shots inside the arc that did find the bottom of the net proved to be just enough in the 116-113 win, their fifth in a row.

While Curry's free throw streak came to an end at 51, he also extended another with his 34-point performance. At age 38 and 233 days, only two older players in NBA history have dropped 30 three games in a row: LeBron James and Michael Jordan.

One miss in a vast ocean of splashes, of course, did nothing to shake Curry's confidence.

"I'm shocked," Curry smirked, "when I miss anytime."

#2972. Russell Wilson interviews for NC State coaching job in major twist amid Philip Rivers flirtation

The North Carolina State football head coach opening might not be Philip Rivers' to lose after all.

ESPN's Adam Schefter reported Tuesday that 10-time NFL Pro Bowler Russell Wilson interviewed for the Wolfpack job, adding another big name to their coaching search.

Over the weekend, On3 reported that Rivers had emerged as the focus of their coaching search following the firing of Dave Doeren after a 31-28 loss to Stanford that dropped the team to 3-4 overall and 1-3 in ACC play before their win over California under interim coach Todd Goebbel on Saturday.

New York Giants quarterback Russell Wilson (3) warms up before a game against the Philadelphia Eagles in Philadelphia, Sunday, Oct. 26, 2025. for the NY POST

At the time, On3 added that there was no timetable for Rivers' potential hiring, as he intended to finish the high school football season as head coach of St. Michael Catholic in Fairhope, Ala.

Rivers played four years at the school, and his son, Gunner, a four-star quarterback, committed to join the Wolfpack in 2027.

New York Giants quarterback Russell Wilson speaks to the media in East Rutherford, N.J., Monday, Jan. 5, 2026. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

"Ton of respect and appreciation for coach Doeren and all he did for the University," Rivers told AL.com. "I understand the chatter that's out there, but we are getting ready for Foley and the playoffs."

The Wolfpack went 98-74 over 14 seasons under Doeren.

Like Rivers, Wilson spent time in college at NC State, playing three seasons in Raleigh before heading to Wisconsin for his senior year.

Philip Rivers of the Indianapolis Colts walks to the locker room during a game against the Houston Texans at NRG Stadium in Houston, Texas, Jan. 4, 2026. Getty Images

Earlier this year, Wilson joined CBS' "NFL Today" pregame show alongside James Brown, Nate Burleson, and Bill Cowher.

Wilson started three games for the Giants at the start of last season before losing the job to Jaxson Dart.

He flirted with the Jets over the offseason and told The Post he had an offer from another team, but decided to get behind the mic instead for the 2026 season.

Wilson has no previous coaching experience, but his 13 NFL seasons, which included a Super Bowl championship with the Seahawks, could make him an intriguing candidate for a program looking to turn things around.

#2968. Q&A with Hall of Famer Mitch Richmond, talking Yaxel Lendeborg, LeBron, Jokic, Game Pulse

Mitch Richmond is NBA history personified: NBA champion, Rookie of the Year, five-time All-NBA, six-time All-Star and Hall of Famer. For many fans, he is best remembered as part of the legendary Run TMC era of the Golden State Warriors, with Tim Hardaway and Chris Mullin.

Before sitting down for an interview with NBC Sports, Richmond was interacting with fans on the NBA App through Game Pulse. For those unfamiliar, Game Pulse combines a live court visualization with highlights and advanced statistics, including shot charts, in a live-updating experience that also features commentary and perspectives from NBA legends such as Muggsy Bogues, Carlos Boozer, Gary Payton, Joakim Noah and more.Here is our conversation with Richmond (edited for length and clarity).

NBC Sports: You've been on this Game Pulse NBA app experience with the fans. How has that been for you?

Richmond: It's been great. You know, that's my first time being on the Pulse. I always watch the association, and sometimes I already catch everything on NBA TV. But it was my first time, so it was a good experience.

Q: It seems like it's a good experience for the fans. They get real interaction not just with the studio people, but with people like yourself, who happen to be Hall of Famers.

Richmond: Yeah, no question about it. It's just the fan engagement. The fans want to be as close as they can, and sometimes they want to hear from other players watching the game. So it gives me an opportunity to kind of reach out to them, and especially since I played so long ago, so they don't know me. But it's a time to start Googling me. You know what I mean?

Q: I think people underestimate, though, like how great and how impactful Run TMC was, and that era of Warriors basketball. As you look at the league now. Is there a player that kind of reminds you of Tim Hardaway?

Richmond: That's a good question. Oh man, who reminds me of Little Tim? Oh man, I don't, it's hard. He had such power when he dribbles the ball, he gives you that shoulder as he goes by that you can't get back in front of him. Yeah, man, that's hard to do.

Q: It might be even harder with Chris Mullin in some ways.

Richmond: Oh man, let's see who Mully would be. I mean, Mully was not the fastest guy, but it is back to what I say: It's how you use your body, and how you use your skills, and how you move. We see it now in today's game, it's all about getting guys in angles when they pick-and-roll. They want you on their back and they want to make sure that you never get back in front of them. Mully had the tendency, if you get on the side of him, he has a way that you never get back around him. And so he was really, really methodical of how he's going to move and take his little steps, and might do a little bird flip. So yeah, he was very tough to guard.

Q: You're close to the Warriors still to this day. They've been fun to watch at the start of the season. What's impressed you about Yaxel Lendeborg as a rookie, just coming in like this?

Richmond: Coming in with that attitude, man, like I belong. And truly, truly, they need a spark like that. We don't know how long Curry is gonna Curry is gonna give us, but they need a young star to come in and kind of help some of that load for Curry right now. I think he's the guy. He has the temperament, and I like him. I like how he came in with Luka, arguing a little bit, but hey, I think that you need that. You need that spark. You need that spice.

Q: I like that [Warriors coach Steve] Kerr trusted him enough to give him that assignment in his first NBA game.

Richmond: I'm pretty sure he wanted it, and I think that's what great players do at times, man. It's for greatness. I want to guard the best player. You know what I mean. I have no problem. I want to guard the best player. We haven't seen that in a while.

Q: What other rookies have stood out to you?

Richmond: I mean, you look at Caleb. Man, he's going to be a star down in Chicago. Caleb Williams. I like AJ [Dybantsa of the Wizards]. AJ is good. Even the young [Cameron] Boozer. Hopefully, he can start to get his game rolling. But I think it's a lot of good young talent that we have, and I just love watching the games, to tell you the truth.

Q: Houston is off to a fast start. Dallas has knocked off two undefeated teams. What are you liking about those teams? What have you seen from them that's making them look kind of dangerous early?

Richmond: I spent a little time with them in Macau when the Rockets played the Dallas Mavericks over there [preseason], and so they looked like just a fun team. And actually, both teams was personable, and I think they get along with one another. One, and they got two good coaches that's coaching them right now. So, man, I thought Houston had a little bit more than Dallas, but Dallas has been playing excellent this year.

Q: Houston, I like the way they're built. I think they can be really dangerous in a West that feels a little more open right now.

Richmond: No question about it. I think the West is wide open. I think someone... can Portland come out of there? You know Portland is looking pretty good right now. I think the West, like you say, the West is wide open. Lakers are even looking pretty good.

Q: You mentioned Portland. Is there anybody else who's just like, wow! When they're on, I kind of want to watch them. I want to watch those players.

Richmond: I'm loving watching the Knicks, tell you the truth. I love watching the Knicks and how they play. Then I just start going for players to see how they're doing. I go and pop over at Denver and see what [Nikola] Jokic is doing. I'm always on the channel with Golden State. I'm always turning the channel to Dallas. I mean, the Lakers, because the Lakers can actually do something as well. We didn't expect that, but the center that they got from Utah?

Richmond: I mean, no one ever saw this, buried in Utah, and now seeing him, and he was hurt as well. But to see what he's been doing and the numbers he's been putting up, you know, they needed a big man like that to run the floor and catch it at the rim.

Q: On the other side, the Heat have just not gelled yet. It hasn't come together consistently. When you see them, what's missing?

Richmond: I think just a little bit more time together. You know what I mean? I think sometimes, teams mesh very well. Look at Philly. You know, how LeBron [James] just came and and haven't missed the beat over there. They're just playing real fluid ball as well. I think Boston is playing pretty good as well. I think sometimes you need just a little bit more time to gel. We haven't seen that yet, so hopefully they can they can catch a groove and start winning some games.

Q: Philadelphia has gelled really quickly, but is that just LeBron bringing his own culture and that's a team where guys seem willing to sacrifice, and they're kind of in the right place to make plays.

Richmond: I think the sacrifice is a little different when you have a star player coming in that is a volume shooter. LeBron kind of plays every part of the game, so he doesn't need those 40-point nights. You know, he might have one this year, but he has enough guns around him. And I think the guys, you have your shooting hands ready to shoot the ball when you have a guy that is a willing passer, and LeBron has been more of a willing passer, especially when he knows that he got young guns at his beck and call anywhere he goes. These guys can really shoot the ball. Look at Embiid. Embiid is in better shape. The guys are bringing enthusiasm. They had some enthusiasm over there. Let's see if they can keep it the whole year, because at some point in time, teams are going to start figuring them out, and see how it happens. But I do like the start of them; they look like a team to be reckoned with later.

Q: You mentioned Jokic's passing and a couple other guys. There are some great passers in the league right now. Who are some of the guys you want to tune in to watch because they see the court differently?

Richmond: Jokic is that guy. You know, if I played with him, man, I'd be coming off screens all day, going back door because he truly, truly finds guys. We have to put LeBron, he's still there. LeBron still knows how to get passes to the right guys. It's a few others out there, but I just love how guys like that move the ball, share it, and it looks wonderful.

#2961. How confident are you in the Bears and in Caleb Williams moving forward?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NFL. Throughout the year, we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bears fans and fans across the country.

The Chicago Bears have won three straight games in the most impressive fashion imaginable. Caleb Williams is the 1st quarterback in Bears history to lead his team to 30+ points in three consecutive games with no turnovers. The Bears also never trailed in any of these three games, and that 3-game streak of scoring 30 points while never trailing is tied for the second-longest streak in franchise history. They went four in a row three times, all in the 1940s (1941, 1942, and 1948). Those teams were led by Hall of Fame quarterback Sid Luckman, who is the only quarterback to win a league MVP for the Bears.

There was some buzz around Williams as an MVP candidate before the season, and that was amplified following the Bears' 59-point explosion in Week 1, but the hamstring injury seemed to derail that because it kept him out of the next three games.

In his weekly NFL MVP Tracker at NFL.com, Kevin Patra wrote about the historical unlikelihood of Williams winning it.

In the post NFL-AFL merger era (since 1970) in non-strike-shortened seasons, only one player has missed three games and still won the NFL MVP award: Joe Montana in 1989. Cool Joe battled through various injuries that season, missing Weeks 6, 8, and 15, and portions of others.

While a long shot, the Bears did just beat last year's Super Bowl participants in back-to-back weeks on primetime. They also have three more stand-alone games on the schedule, including a third straight primetime affair this Sunday.

Patra also explained why Montana's performance in 1989 won it that year and how Caleb could have a similar path.

Despite missing three games, Montana was the best QB on the best team in the NFL, winning the MVP vote in a landslide (62 of 70 votes). The 49ers QB overcame the lack of counting stats by leading the NFL in all the efficiency categories: 70.2 percent completion rate, a 6.7 TD% rate, 53.7% success rate, 9.1 yards per attempt average, 270.8 yards per game, and set the then-record with a 112.4 passer rating — when 100 passer ratings were uncommon.

That's Williams' path to the MVP: Be the most efficient passer on the best team.

Caleb may not hit gaudy passing yard or touchdown numbers, but if all his other numbers are among the best in the NFL, while the Bears keep winning games, how could he not be a finalist?

#2959. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2957. ‘I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers

'I'm finding my footing': Jaylen Brown's role taking shape with Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Jaylen Brown has spent the opening weeks of the season describing the Sixers as a work in progress.

He offered a similarly humble perspective Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena. His performance, however, provided the clearest look yet at how he can influence this group while sharing the floor with the club's other established stars.

Brown finished with 25 points, 11 rebounds and five assists in the Sixers' 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls. He shot 9 for 15 from the field, made four three-pointers and did not commit a turnover.

Five of his rebounds came on the offensive end, an area Brown has emphasized while the Sixers continue piecing together their frontcourt rotation.

"Just a mindset," Brown said. "Just trying to create extra opportunities for my team. As we're trying to patch together our fives and our front line, I try to make an extra contribution and emphasis on the glass."

Brown has also been careful about how he describes his place on a roster that already belonged to Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey.

Maxey scored 15 of his 26 points in the fourth quarter Monday, continuing his torrid start to the season. He has led the Sixers in scoring while being the only starter to appear in all eight games.

Brown is not interested in interrupting that.

"Maxey's been leading the way, 100 percent," Brown said. "He's been here. This is his team. Him and Joel have been here for a while, so the mindset has been to complement those guys. I'm finding my footing, but Maxey has been leading the way for sure."

Brown's footing looked firm against Chicago.

Beyond his scoring, he helped the Sixers control the glass, moved the ball within an offense that generated 23 assists during the first half and supplied the physicality Nick Nurse expects from him.

"The dude is tough and he wins," Nurse said. "I'll take that all day long. The degree of difficulty on some of the shots he hit was really amazing. I thought he pounded the glass and got some key rebounds. Makes a lot of winning plays. Man, he's a winner."

Brown spent the first 10 years of his career in Boston. He won a championship there, reached another NBA Finals and grew from a 20-year-old prospect into one of the league's premier two-way wings.

Moving into a new system alongside James, Embiid and Maxey was never going to be seamless.

"I mean, he's fitting in along with all of us," James said. "We're getting better and better. It's not going to happen overnight. He spent his first 10 years in a system in Boston, in the city, so it's going to take some adjustment.

"It takes adjustment for him. It's going to take adjustment for the rest of the guys that joined this franchise as well. But tonight was a great step."

James pointed to Brown's "pressure, his shot-making ability, his rebounding ability" and his professionalism.

Brown's early influence has extended beyond his production.

The Sixers entered Monday with one of the NBA's best half-court defenses. Brown has been one of the group's most persistent voices, stressing communication, preparation and attention to opposing players' tendencies.

"That's the part that I care about the most as a group, not individually," Brown said. "Having a mentality night in and night out, respecting your opponent, respecting the game, making sure that we are executing our coverages, we're being clear in our communication and then we're also taking away tendencies on a night-to-night basis.

"Those things I just explained are over 50 percent of the battle."

Brown understands that maintaining those details across an 82-game schedule will require more than reminders.

"A little bit of accountability," he said. "You learn different guys require different things. How you communicate with each guy may be a little different, but accountability for sure and confrontation if need be.

"The offense is going to be what it's going to be, but our defense has got to be non-negotiable. That's something I say all the time. Non-negotiable. You want to be a good team, defense is non-negotiable."

The Sixers showed both sides of their defensive potential Monday.

They built a double-digit lead during the first half, allowed Chicago to score 36 points in the second quarter and then created separation after halftime. Philadelphia held the Bulls to 20 points in the third quarter before pulling away in the fourth.

Brown did not celebrate their ability to raise their intensity after it slipped.

"I don't like that we have that," he said. "It's good that we have shown the potential, but we've got to keep that switch on. It doesn't work like that where you just flip the switch.

"It can catch up to us and it can bite us against a better team or on a night that we're not being as crisp on offense. It can't be a switch just because we're playing a lesser opponent or whatever the case may be. We've got to make sure that mentality is from the beginning to end. Can't skip any steps."

That urgency accompanies considerable optimism.

Brown said the Sixers enjoy playing together. He loves when James grabs a rebound because "he's like a quarterback," capable of launching an outlet pass or immediately pushing the pace.

The group is learning when to attack, when to move the ball and how to avoid hesitating in its effort to play unselfishly.

"It's a lot of talent, man," Brown said. "We like each other, and we're just kind of building that chemistry of how we can get this thing to a well-oiled machine. I think we're taking steps forward every single day."

The Sixers are 7-1 despite Brown believing those steps remain incomplete. They have won close games, erased sizable deficits and received important contributions from different players.

Brown's performance Monday showed another version of how their pieces can fit.

"We still have a long way to go to being a really, really solid, great team," Brown said. "But we've been able to win some games. We started out the season great, 7-1, and you haven't seen our best version yet.
