#2922. 15 players to watch amid NFL trade deadline buzz

Take a look at the NFL standings. There's no sure thing this season.

Once the Los Angeles Rams got off to a rocky start, it opened things up around the league. There is no runaway team this season, and there is also a group of teams already looking ahead to 2027. That could lead to a ton of activity before the NFL's trade deadline, which is on Nov. 10. There has been plenty of action well ahead of the deadline, with players like guard Quenton Nelson going to Seattle, defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons landing in Chicago, defensive tackle DeForest Buckner heading to San Francisco, cornerback Joey Porter Jr. being shipped to Dallas and receiver Marvin Harrison Jr. being dealt to Houston.

That's already a lot of big trades and we're just getting started. Here are the 15 names to watch between now and the deadline:

There was a quarterback change in Arizona on Sunday and it will stick. Brissett was removed late in the third quarter for Gardner Minshew, who led a wild comeback win over the Cowboys in overtime. The Cardinals announced Monday that Minshew will start Week 9 with rookie Carson Beck being the backup.

All of a sudden, the Cardinals have a third quarterback who could be moved. Minshew can handle the starting job as well as the struggling Brissett was doing, and Beck will get starts at some point. Teams don't usually trade their backup quarterback, but Brissett isn't even the backup anymore and is expendable. And he has plenty of starting experience, though his $15.5 million salary for this season would have to be managed to swing a deal with most teams. He's the most likely quarterback to move at the deadline for any team desperate for a stopgap starter (perhaps the Ravens?). – Frank Schwab

If we take the Browns at their reported word, Sanders is not likely to be traded at the deadline. You can never say never until the deadline passes, but for now it appears Cleveland will continue to carry four quarterbacks, which includes Sanders. He has been QB2 behind Deshaun Watson and has replaced Watson for a handful of snaps after Watson got hurt. Watson was sacked eight times Sunday, and the 5-3 Browns might fear a more serious Watson injury as they sit just outside the current playoff picture after Week 8's defeat to the Steelers.

Given his cost-controlled salary, low draft pedigree and Cleveland's crowded QB room, Sanders might normally be considered a higher-likelihood trade candidate. So far there has been little apparent traction. We'll see if that changes over the next week. Perhaps there's even a world where Sanders is in the mix to start in Cleveland if the free-agent-to-be Watson is not brought back in 2027. – Eric Edholm

The Eagles reportedly won't trade Barkley. That also doesn't make much sense. Philadelphia is going nowhere, Barkley is turning 30 years old in February, his production has declined since 2024 and the Eagles have never been overly sentimental about player moves. Barkley brought great memories and was the superstar on a Super Bowl champion, but the Eagles should listen to calls. They could pass if teams try to get Barkley cheap, given his current injuries, but it seems like he'd have more value to a contending team this season than the Eagles going forward.

Maybe the Eagles are serious and Barkley is indeed off the table. That won't stop speculation. It makes a ton of sense for Philadelphia to move Barkley now. That's why we're still keeping an eye on Barkley as the deadline approaches. If they don't move him, that decision will be discussed not only at the deadline but beyond. – Frank Schwab

It would be a surprise if Taylor isn't traded, after the Colts sent off guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner. The question is who lands Taylor and his 8,208 career rushing yards. He's still one of the NFL's best runners and plenty of teams have a need. The Giants, Broncos, Packers and Vikings top the list. While other backs are likely available, nobody else would pop like Taylor. Other players might be more prudent from a football aspect (Jeffery Simmons to the Bears, most notably) but no player acquisition would generate more buzz than nabbing Taylor. – Frank Schwab

Pollard has said he doesn't want to be traded, and the Titans reportedly have rebuffed offers, but a 1-7 record and a reasonable proposal could change their minds. He's in the final year of a three-year deal, makes fairly modest starting RB money and would provide the Titans with some salary cap relief if they moved him.

Pollard has averaged a robust 4.5 yards per carry, has back-to-back 100-yard rushing outings (and five TDs in two games) and remains in his relative prime at age 29 with fewer than 2,000 career touches. On paper, he checks every box for a sale candidate if the Titans want to add more draft assets before the deadline. And for teams looking for RB help, he might come far cheaper than some of the other players mentioned above. – Eric Edholm

The memories of Thomas' rookie season might last forever. They could lead to some team thinking it can revive his career. Thomas was a first-round draft pick in 2024 and had 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns. He looked like a future star. In the season and a half since he has fewer than 1,000 yards. He has fallen well out of the Jaguars' hierarchy at receiver, leading to plenty of trade speculation. The Jaguars, who look like Super Bowl contenders, could keep Thomas just in case there are injuries at receiver. But teams like the Seahawks, Chiefs or Commanders would likely be interested, hoping Thomas rediscovers his rookie form in a new situation. – Frank Schwab

Interestingly, Jennings was rumored to be a target of the 49ers, trying to bring him back into the fold, but declined the offer, saying he didn't want to go back to San Francisco. That's especially interesting considering his best production came with the 49ers, notching a 975-yard season in 2024 and a nine-TD campaign a year ago before leaving on a one-year deal in Minnesota. Jennings has been almost a ghost with the Vikings, with only six catches in his six games and only modest upticks in his activity lately.

The Vikings need all the offense they can find, but Jennings hasn't been a great fit. If they can include him in a deal for a running back or defensive depth, the Vikings might consider it. – Eric Edholm

Downs has requested a trade, saying he's "open to whatever" when it comes to moving on from the Colts. The team has already kick-started its offloading efforts with Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, and don't appear to have strong intentions to keep Downs. The 25-year-old receiver is in the final year of his rookie deal and has stepped up as the Colts' leading option with Alec Pierce hurt. Downs doesn't fit a WR1 profile but could be a quality complement for a team seeking an upgrade to its unit, similar to the Seahawks' deadline trade for Rashid Shaheed last year.

Downs is a smaller slot receiver and might be a fit for only certain teams, but with his trade request and the seeming league-wide need for WR help, he figures to be a candidate who possibly moves on soon. – Eric Edholm

Jeudy has always been mercurial. He came into the NFL as a first-round draft pick and was fine early on with the Broncos, but wore out his welcome and was traded to the Browns. With the Browns, he had a stellar 2024, with 1,229 yards and a Pro Bowl nod. But he has dipped again and with the Browns turning toward rookies Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion, Jeudy has just 171 yards this season. Still, Jeudy has one of the biggest plays of the Browns' 5-3 start, a 33-yard catch against the Ravens to set up a game-winning field goal.

Jeudy, 27, could be an intriguing gamble for a team that needs juice at receiver. He's no sure thing, but the talent is there. – Frank Schwab

Mack isn't the same player he was when he was a first-team All-Pro three times in four years. His peak might lead him to the Pro Football Hall of Fame. He's 35 years old now, but still is an effective player. And with the 0-7 Chargers going nowhere, it would make sense for the team to move on and allow Mack a chance at playoff success in what might be the final season of his career. Mack is in his 13th season and has never been part of a playoff win.

Mack would add leadership and a strong edge defender down the stretch for a contender. Mack has 8.5 sacks in 19 games over the past two seasons, including three in seven games this season. Given his age, he might not cost that much. A reunion with the Bears seems like the perfect fit. – Frank Schwab

When the Eagles drafted Smith in 2023, draft experts lost their minds. They gave the Eagles a consensus grade point average of 4.12 — better than an A. While the Eagles' first pick, defensive tackle Jalen Carter, worked out very well, Smith has not.

Smith has just 14.5 career sacks but still has the draft pedigree and enticing athleticism. The Eagles picked up Smith's fifth-year option for 2027, which means they won't be looking to give him away. But for a floundering Eagles team, the idea of recouping some value on Smith during his fourth lukewarm season has to be a possibility.

There are a lot of Eagles on this list. Philadelphia becomes the seller to watch as the trade deadline approaches. For some contender looking for a shot on a sleeper, taking a stab at Smith at age 25 could pay off nicely. – Frank Schwab

Sweat reportedly requested a trade in August, and the Cardinals very publicly said they had no interest in honoring that request. Could they have a different mindset now? Arizona has won two of three and is a fairly respectable 3-5, albeit in a very tough division the Cardinals are unlikely to win. Sweat's play has not been as impactful as a year ago, when he notched 12 sacks and four forced fumbles, but he had a huge sack in overtime of Dak Prescott in Dallas on Sunday.

Speaking of Dallas, the Cowboys figure to be among the teams that could have interest in Sweat, along with the Packers, where Jonathan Gannon (his former coach) is defensive coordinator. Sweat's contract kicks up to $23.6 million salary cap hits each of the next two seasons, which puts him outside the top-10 edge rusher deals, but there have been rumors of Sweat wanting a new deal after this season, which could complicate things. – Eric Edholm

There are conflicting reports on the 25-year-old Ojomo and whether the Eagles might consider dangling him. Normally, they'd want to build around an emerging defensive tackle who has started the past three games and been more productive recently than highly paid DTs Jalen Carter and Jordan Davis. But Ojomo is set to hit free agency, and it's unlikely the Eagles can afford him after what they paid Carter and Davis. If the Eagles have designs on re-signing Ojomo, they'll have to be creative. They are 2-6, losers of six straight, including back-to-back thumpings in prime time, and QB Jalen Hurts (along with others) is on injured reserve. The Jeffery Simmons trade to Chicago had to be an eye-opener for Philly, value-wise, and might cause the team to consider moving him before the deadline. – Eric Edholm

Baker has been tremendous for the Cardinals over his 10 seasons with the team, making eight Pro Bowls. But it might be time for a rebuilding club to move on. Baker can provide playmaking at secondary, as he has for a decade. He is signed through 2027, with a reasonable salary of a little more than $16 million for next season. So he wouldn't be a short-term rental for a team.

It would sting for the Cardinals to move on from Baker, one of the team's best players and only positive lights in the post-Larry Fitzgerald era. But it's prudent for a team looking for the future. And it would be a big get for a contending club that needs a difference maker in the secondary. – Frank Schwab

Following his three-pick game Sunday against Joe Burrow, Hooker is the NFL's INT leader with five, and his 114 return yards off those picks also is first. The 28-year-old veteran has quietly carved out a nice career over eight seasons with the Titans and is enjoying one of his better years, tackling and covering well. The Titans signed him to an extension last summer through the 2028 season with a fairly reasonable salary outside the top 10 safeties.

Given their 1-7 record at the bye, the Titans could offload more vets after trading Jeffery Simmons. – Eric Edholm

#2921. The next wave of luxury real estate: marina developments with yacht parking

Marinas are becoming the next big draw for luxury real estate, as the global wealthy search for homes to park their ever-larger yachts, according to developers.

With the number of yachts surging since Covid-19, real estate developers and investors are rushing to fill the shortage of boat slips and marina communities. There are now over 6,000 superyachts over 30 meters (98 feet) around the world, and another 200 delivered every year, according to SuperYacht Times.

The growth in the size and number of yachts has created a shortage of yacht slips. And investors are rushing to fill demand. Last year, Blackstone paid $5.6 billion for Safe Harbor Marinas, which then paid $1.5 billion in August for 65 marinas and storage sites purchased from MarineMax. European private equity firms are also on a marina buying spree to fill the shortage in the Mediterranean.

Real estate developers now see marinas as a powerful magnet for ultra-wealthy buyers, especially in South Florida. Billionaire Ken Griffin is building a megayacht marina in Miami with eight megayacht slips, including one for his 308-foot yacht.

The largest and most ambitious is Related Group's $2 billion new marina development in Fort Lauderdale. Slated to break ground next year and open in 2030, the project will include a St. Regis Hotel, condos, a beach club and slips for yachts up to 350 feet long.

"Here in South Florida is there's been a scarcity of yachting destinations and marinas and slips," said Related Group's Nick Pérez. "So when you're able to combine irreplaceable real estate and a marina, you just have a winning formula."

The 40-acre development will include a St. Regis hotel with 122 rooms, a 78-residence condo tower, 106 resort residences, as well as a deepwater marina that can hold 250 boats. It will also include a sports and wellness club with a padel and tennis court, yoga lawn, fitness center and pools. And it will have its own beach club.

Fort Lauderdale has long been the yachting capital of the U.S., with a vast infrastructure of yacht repair and renovation companies, along with crew, brokers, charter experts and shipbuilder offices. An estimated 2,000 superyachts pass through Fort Lauderdale each year, according to tourism officials. Many yacht owners are looking for real estate not only for themselves but also their crew.

Pérez said the new St. Regis development will not only appeal to the yachting community but also to a growing number of international buyers who are looking for a lower-cost alternative to Palm Beach and Miami. Several buyers from Mexico and Brazil have already purchased in the building.

He said up to 30% of the buyers at the project could be international.

"Fort Lauderdale is no longer T-shirt shops and flip-flops, it's now a luxury destination, with national retailers like Rolex, Patek, Cartier," Pérez said. "When you look at Miami and Palm Beach and the growth, now it's Fort Lauderdale's time to shine."

While boat slips are the main attraction, many buyers in Fort Lauderdale are pouring in from Southern California and the Northeast to take advantage of Florida's lower taxes.

"With the taxation situation in California, we're seeing a lot of interest from Newport and the Laguna area and San Diego," Pérez said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2918. Sen. Chris Murphy still hospitalized after suffering a stroke over the weekend

Sen. Chris Murphy remained hospitalized Tuesday after suffering a stroke over the weekend, his office confirmed.

"He's continuing to improve and heartened by the outpouring of well wishes," Josh Sorbe, Murphy's communications director, told CNBC in an emailed statement. "He early voted in Connecticut, and he encourages everyone to make their voices heard today by voting and sending a message in this defining election."

His office did not offer additional details when asked about his status or his expected recovery.

The Senate is due back in session Monday following Tuesday's midterm elections.

Murphy began experiencing stroke symptoms on Saturday while at his son's hockey game, his chief of staff Allison Herwitt said in a statement Sunday.

"He was taken to a local hospital, where it was discovered he had experienced a stroke. He was transferred to Johns Hopkins for further monitoring and is responding well to treatment," Herwitt said.

Herwitt said at the time that Murphy was "awake and alert."

Johns Hopkins Hospital's main campus is in Baltimore, though it has other locations in and around Washington.

Murphy is not up for reelection on Tuesday but had been traveling extensively this fall in support of Democratic candidates. Murphy came to the Senate in 2013 after a six-year stint in the U.S. House. He's also been mentioned as a potential 2028 presidential candidate.

#2917. Ravens starting undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano at QB for ‘Thursday Night Football’

In what's been a roller-coaster season for the Ravens, they're going with a largely untested rookie under center on Thursday night.

With injuries to two-time MVP Lamar Jackson and backup Tyler Huntley, head coach Jesse Minter decided on Monday that rookie Joe Fagnano will get the start against the Jaguars.

Jackson went down on the first offensive snap in Sunday's loss to the Bills with a thumb injury, and Huntley later exited with an injury of his own.

Baltimore Ravens quarterback Joe Fagnano (12) throws a pass as Buffalo Bills linebacker Terrel Bernard (8) attempts a tackle on Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Orchard Park, N.Y. Getty Images

That thrust Fagnano, an undrafted rookie out of UConn, into the quarterback role. He went 10-for-21 passing with 91 yards and an interception while taking three sacks in relief.

Baltimore, earlier Tuesday, signed QB Kedon Slovis off the Packers' practice squad and added him to the active roster. Austin Reed was added to Baltimore's practice squad as the de facto third signal-caller.

Fagnano was also banged up during the loss to Buffalo and wore a brace on his ankle at Tuesday's practice, ESPN reported.

Nonetheless, Fagnano is thrilled to be getting his first start as a professional.

"It's special," Fagnano told reporters. "It's really the only way I can think about it."

Baltimore Ravens quarterback Joe Fagnano (12) speaks at a press conference after an NFL football game against the Buffalo Bills in Orchard Park, N.Y., Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026. AP Photo/Sue Ogrocki

Minter said he believes in Fagnano's composure under center and that he can handle the pressure of going against a 5-2 Jacksonville squad.

"He's cool, calm and collected; doesn't really get rattled, has a really good demeanor, played in a bunch of games in college. So, you certainly feel good about it," Minter said.

"I thought we operated really well when he was in there in the preseason, just from time on the play clock and penalties and substitutions. So, I am excited to see him go out there Thursday."

Fagnano, as UConn's full-time starter last season, threw for 3,448 yards and 28 touchdowns, both of which led independent schools.

#2916. Fantasy Football Start 'Em, Sit 'Em Week 9: TE Edition

If you need TE production and don't have a consensus top-12 Week 9 fantasy tight end, here are three highly unconventional, high-upside bargains. Also, if you're planning to start one of those previously referenced top-12 fantasy TEs, these three are riskier than usual.

I've hit 12 of my last 18 highly unconventional picks and want to keep it going with some world-class tight ends.

As always, we'll use FantasyPros' Week 9 composite rankings as our guide.

Start - Brevyn Spann-Ford, Dallas Cowboys (Ranked 36th)

Is Brevyn Spann-Ford a better fantasy bet than teammate Jake Ferguson for the rest of the season? It's hard to argue with what we've all seen over the past few weeks. A healthy Spann-Ford is as good a dart throw as any non-top-16 TE.

Start - Eli Raridon, New England Patriots (Ranked 25th)

Rookie Eli Raridon is high-risk, but has decent TD upside. Hunter Henry exited early last week, so we don't know whether he'll be 100% in Week 9. Still, Drake Maye has looked Raridon's way 16 times in the past four weeks. If you don't have a nearly automatic top-12 TE, Raridon is worth a shot.

Start - Greg Dulcich, Miami Dolphins (Ranked 17th)

Although Greg Dulcich isn't a great bet to score in Miami's bottom-tier offense, he's become a consistently favored target for Malik Willis. Combine that with a matchup against the high-powered Lions, and it's easy to envision Dulcich cracking double digits.

Sit - Travis Kelce, Kansas City Chiefs (Ranked 11th)

As always, recommending sitting a top-12 TE is a tough business. But I'll embrace it once again. I've tagged Travis Kelce as overvalued over the past two weeks. With Rashee Rice back in full swing, Kelce probably will need to score to get 10+ points. Look elsewhere for higher ceilings.

Sit - Tucker Kraft, Green Bay Packers (Ranked 10th)

In my 14-team fantasy league, I traded for Tucker Kraft in September and have been pretty happy with his output since then. But this week I'm benching him in favor of T.J. Hockenson. Kraft faces a Patriots defense that allows only 6.5 fantasy points per game to opposing tight ends. As with Kelce, he probably needs to score to be a must-start option, and I believe that will be tough on New England's home turf, where the Patriots have thrived.

Sit - Harold Fannin, Cleveland Browns (Ranked 6th)

Harold Fannin simply crushed it last week after I declared him "hit-or-miss." Well, Fannin won that battle. But he was also the offensive focal point on a team that's had many focal points, including (from week to week) K.C. Concepcion, Denzel Boston, Jerry Jeudy, and Quinshon Judkins. Experts are ranking him highly partly because of what he just did, not based on the context of who flopped because of it.

This article originally appeared on Touchdown Wire: Fantasy Football Start 'Em, Sit 'Em Week 9: TE Edition

#2915. What We Learned from the Spurs win over the Jazz

Somewhere in the second quarter, with the Spurs up one and about to be up more, I realized I wasn't watching the game. I was, instead, watching myself not watch the game, which is a worse hobby and the one I'm apparently best at. The game was fine. The game was exactly what it said on the label: a nondescript tilt between two teams already headed down different tracks. The shine of October basketball had worn off completely and, like summer turning to fall, the Grind had arrived.

As a first quarter blowout turned into a 2nd quarter snoozer, my brain began to wander a bit. I could be anywhere in the world right now. A plastic stool in Hanoi, a bowl of pho, a little light rain. A mountain pass in the Andes, thin air, nobody asking me for a take. Man, I should be reading books. Seeing films. I should be the kind of person whose passport has all those cool stamps in it. This one precious life the universe handed me was unraveling in real time as Ace Bailey stepped into a 25-foot pull-up three to cut the Jazz's deficit to one.

Then Stephon Castle missed a layup and I almost decided to call it a night. It was as annoying as it was unremarkable and I just didn't need it anymore. I reached for the remote and, as I did, Victor Wembanyama, who a minute earlier had similarly flubbed an alley-oop, grabbed the rebound and tipped it back in. Twenty seconds after Bailey's three. No celebration. Barely a facial expression.

I'd love to tell you that's the moment I'd been looking for, but that would be too tidy. What I'd been searching for was a hook. A story, a cause, a catalyst. Anything to hold off the particular ennui of being a fan of a good team. And Wemby wasn't even good last night, not by any stretch. Five of fifteen. Zero for five from three. Five turnovers. He was in that mode he gets in from time to time where he decides the Key to Victory is to simply chuck it.

The Spurs won by 22 anyway, not because of a miracle but because this roster has more than one way to be competent. The Spurs are, mercifully, more than the sum of their parts.

Not that the parts are bad. Quite the contrary. Dylan Harper scored all 17 of his points before the third quarter was half over. Stephon Castle is an animal. De'Aaron Fox looks like the guy we paid for. Tarris Reed continues to grab rebounds in a way that feels like it's going to be important (seven in about seven minutes last night). Every wing combination we've thrown on the court is its own kind of fun.

I mean, look at Harrison Barnes. A man as old as time itself (he's four years younger than me) had an insanely active night. He knocked down threes from 29, 25 and 24 feet, he ran around, he caused trouble. Early in the fourth he got tangled up with Brice Sensabaugh, slid across the floor, got back up, and just stared at him while the refs reviewed it in slow motion. I'm told that clip racked up 140,000 views on the world wide web within about an hour.

The thing about the Grind is that it doesn't have to mean anything, which means that everything within the Grind gets to be exactly what it is. A tip dunk. A well executed outlet pass. An old man staring down Brice Sensabaugh. Like, I didn't come out of the womb craving the Larry O'Brien trophy. I just was at my breakfast table in 1996 watching Michael Jordan dunk on Rik Smits during a SportsCenter rerun, and some part of me said, with zero strategic vision, "Yes. More of that." More of that was the entire ask and, frankly, it still is.

Look at us. You and me. Like I said, we could be anywhere in the world right now, but we're right here with the Spurs and the Jazz on a Monday night in November. The stools in Hanoi will still be there someday, but a precocious superstar, having the worst night of his season, is putting a ball back through the hoop like a child tucking in his stuffed animals for the night.

You don't have to impress some greater importance on a moment like that to make it mean something. You were there. It's a part of you now and you got to live it.

If that isn't special, I don't know what is.

Dylan Harper seems almost casually good at basketball. It's remarkable and breathtaking, these things he's doing, but it also has that effect of seeming effortless when he's casually dismantling another team. In the second quarter he stole the ball and in a blink Vassell was dunking off his pass. In the third, he stole it again and Stephon was finishing an alley-oop before I'd even registered it. He added 8 assists and 7 rebounds on top of all his scoring. I find myself almost taking it for granted at this point, and we've really only started to scratch the surface of what he's capable of. Scary to think about! (For our opponents. For us it's just exceedingly cool and fun.)

I know it's just the Jazz, grain of salt and all that, but I never get tired of seeing the Spurs get out on the break and run. They won fast-break points 26-9, against a Jazz team that turned it over just 11 times, a season low for Utah, so a lot of that was pure rebound-and-go. They also won the paint 52-36 and opened the game on an 18-2 run. Run for days my boys. Grab the ball and never stop running. Only good can come from this. Open looks from three, easy paths to the rim, potential foul trouble for the opponents. Push that pace. Push that pace. PUSH THAT PACE.

This is not how development works and, to be clear, I don't know what I'm talking about, but I absolutely crave Tarris Reed minutes. I want him to become a Sasquatch, Godzilla, King Kong, Loch Ness, Goblin, ghoul and a zombie with no conscience. I want basketballs to fear him checking into the game because they're about to get rebounded to within an inch of their lives.

I love a wire-to-wire win. Next time you hear me complaing about a wire to wire win feel free to come to my house and take an ice cold waterloo out of my fridge as payment for my sins.

I'm a man of all seasons, to be clear, but right now, yes, I am currently in a Waterloo phase of my Sparkling Water career.

There is a particular Black Cherry flavor I've grown fond of and I just can't stay away at this point. It scratches this weird nostalgic part of my brain that yearns for a Blue Sky soda I had in my youth. Do you remember Blue Sky soda?

Yeah, the cans were cool. I don't know. I think I had just never experienced the concept of a soda that existed outside the mainstream world of the Coca-Cola and Pepsi family trees, so when I went on vacation and saw this crazy looking can with a cherry sitting on a cloud it just… broke my brain a little bit and I had to have it.

Who could say? I was six years old. I'd left the summer heat in Texas and woken up the next day in a Colorado mountain town sipping an alien soda from heaven. Maybe it was good, maybe it wasn't, but it stirred something deep in my soul that I think I've been searching for ever since.

And Black Cherry Waterloos scratch that itch for you?

They do. Publix also pretty regularly throws them in Buy One Get Ones for me and who am I to turn down a deal like that.

#2914. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2913. CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue

CrowdStrike is betting that it has a solution to rogue artificial intelligence.

The cybersecurity vendor on Tuesday launched an AI kill switch designed to let security teams stop internal AI agents that go rogue, block them from relaunching and cut off their enterprise network access. The tool is built into CrowdStrike's Falcon Guardian platform, launched in September to secure AI agents. CrowdStrike also announced an integration with Anthropic's Claude Cowork.

"Having the ability to control and turn off, and make a decision and decide, I think that's really important for this next chapter of AI adoption," Chief Business Officer Daniel Bernard told CNBC.

A string of recent high-profile cyber incidents has added urgency to calls for some kind of emergency shutdown mechanism.

The Hugging Face breach orchestrated by rogue OpenAI agents in July was particularly alarming for businesses. Last week, Binance disclosed the largest crypto cyberattack on record and hackers have recently targeted hundreds of refrigerators and freezers at supermarkets in France.

Over the summer, U.S. representatives introduced bipartisan legislation that would require AI companies to build shutdown capabilities into their models.

Critics argue, however, that an emergency stop button is far too complicated to implement across multiple systems, data centers and complex AI models.

Bernard said the kill switch operates like a circuit breaker in systems where customers use its Falcon platform. The process can detect internal agent activity but requires a human to decide to cut the agent off and reinstate access.

"If you kill a process at the root, and we have that level of access at the root … it's very binary," Bernard said. "You either kill the AI and the agent, or you don't, and then you report on that."

The kill switch doesn't delete the agent's code but uses connected AI gateways to cut off access to the AI models that influence decisions, CrowdStrike said.

In the age of AI, businesses are moving quickly to adopt autonomous tools, often without the necessary safeguards and protections.

CrowdStrike believes its new tool will let companies stop AI agent 'misbehavior in its tracks, while leaving room for innovation.

The company also announced plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler AI and AI security testing firm Dreadnode.

CrowdStrike and its rivals are rapidly scooping up cybersecurity startups to keep up with increasing security demands. The need for AI security tools has also powered a broader wave of cybersecurity deal-making.

#2911. Watch: Jets set NHL record with 4 goals over final 2:39 to stun Canadiens

Nov. 4 (UPI) -- The Winnipeg Jets scored four goals over the final 2:39 of regulation to stun the Montreal Canadiens and set an NHL record in Montreal.

The four-goal comeback was the latest in league history and propelled the 5-4 overtime triumph Tuesday at Bell Centre. Forward Kyle Connor scored twice for the Jets. Defenseman Dylan Samberg netted the game-winner 90 seconds remaining overtime.

Samberg's score helped the Jets snap a nine-game losing streak in dramatic fashion.

"What a way to end the streak we were on there," Connor told reporters. "We just came into [Monday] with such a good attitude and just tried to live in the present and even manufacture some kind of positive and jump even if it wasn't there."

Defenseman Tyrel Bauer and forward Morgan Barron scored the first two goals of the Jets rally.

"We are going to enjoy this one," Connor said. "You've gotta enjoy the wins in the National Hockey League, especially when you are on a streak like that.

"We are going to try to use this to catapult us and to build upon it."

Canadiens winger Cole Caufield scored twice in the loss. Fellow forwards Josh Anderson and Owen Beck also scored for Montreal. The loss snapped a five-game winning streak for the Canadiens, the third-best team in the Eastern Conference.

"We were very opportunistic on our chances," Canadiens coach Martin St-Louis said. "I think we gave up 80-something shot attempts. ... They were better than we were. You have to give them credit. They weren't winning. You could tell they had a lot of urgency. They didn't quit.

"You have to tip your hat to what they just did."

The Jets edged the Canadiens 87-47 in shot attempts, 47-22 in shots on goal and 20-22 in hits for their first win since Oct. 13.

"Our team, we played hard," Jets coach Scott Arniel said. "I thought we did a lot of really good things for three periods. We talked about defending our way out of this. I thought we did that. When we didn't have the puck, we worked to get it back."

Canadiens goalie Jacob Fowler totaled 42 saves. Jets net minder Stuart Skinner made 18 stops. Forward Mark Scheifele and defenseman Dylan DeMelo logged two assists apiece in the victory.

Beck lit the lamp for the first score of the night 3:05 into the first period. Anderson followed with a second Canadiens goal with 1:42 remaining in the frame. Caufield scored off power players in each of the next two periods. He gave his Canadiens a 4-0 lead with his second goal just 46 seconds into the final frame.

Bauer then started the Jets' rally with a score with 2:39 remaining in regulation. Barron beat Fowler again 42 seconds later. Connor cut the deficit to one with 60 seconds remaining in the third period. He forced overtime when he scored off a power play with 33 seconds remaining.

Gabriel Vilardi picked the pocket of defenseman Lane Hutson to set up the game-winner. He kicked the puck up to fellow forward Adam Lowry outside the left circle. Lowry then fired a pass above the right circle, finding Samberg. The defenseman finished the game with a shot past Fowler's stick and into the left side of the net.

The Jets (5-8-3) will host the Vancouver Canucks (3-13-1) at 7 p.m. EST Thursday in Winnipeg. The Canadiens (9-3-3) will host the Utah Mammoth (10-6-0) at the same time in Montreal.

#2910. Euro zone inflation hits 4% for first time in three years as interest rate hike looms

Inflation in the euro area jumped to a three-year high of 4% in October, a flash estimate showed on Wednesday.

The reading is in line with the expectations of economists polled by Reuters and was up from 3.8% in September.

Officials said the increase was largely due to surging energy costs, with energy inflation rising to 20.8% from 18.9% in September. Europe, a net energy importer, is vulnerable to external energy shocks and has seen prices rise steadily since the start of the Iran war.

October marked the first time inflation in the euro zone has hit or exceeded the 4% threshold since Sept. 2023's reading of 4.3%.

Inflation also varied across the 21 euro zone economies, with Lithuania recording the hottest inflation rate of 6.1% while Malta's was the lowest at 2.3%. All euro zone countries saw inflation hold above the European Central Bank's 2% target.

The euro was up 0.6% against the U.S. dollar on Wednesday morning, although the greenback fell against a basket of major currencies as counting continued in the U.S. midterm elections, while the yield on the 10-year German bund — broadly seen as a benchmark for euro zone bonds — was marginally higher.

Policymakers said underlying inflation and wages were broadly stable, despite inflation sitting at a three-year high.

The European Central Bank kept its key interest rate at 2.5% last month, following a hike of 25 basis points in September.

Shortly before Wednesday's inflation print, markets were pricing in a 71.8% chance of a hike at the ECB's final meeting of the year in December. Following the data release, markets were pricing in more than a 72% chance of rates rising.

Robert Mertens, global capital markets senior analyst at Validus Risk Management, cautioned that "ongoing turmoil in the French bond market is placing constraints on ECB policy" and the bank's capacity to get inflation under control.

"Should we see an easing in French political unrest, the ECB could potentially pursue a more aggressive hiking path," he said. "If the ECB can deploy more aggressive tightening relative to the Federal Reserve, we would see increased Euro support and upside versus the Dollar."

#2907. Apple warns of risks related to AI agents behaving in 'unintended or unpredictable' ways

Apple said in its annual financial filing on Tuesday that AI agents that can act in "unintended or unpredictable ways" could represent a threat to its business by opening the company up to lawsuits or hurting its reputation.

The new risk factors in Apple's filing show the disruptive potential of AI agents, which have rapidly become top of mind for Silicon Valley's most important companies.

"In particular, artificial intelligence agents and other technologies can take actions autonomously, including interacting with internal or external systems that in some cases they may not be authorized to access, executing or altering code, sending communications, and completing transactions," Apple wrote in the filing.

The company added that agents "may behave in unintended or unpredictable ways, including by taking actions that were not directed, instructed or authorized."

Earlier this year, OpenAI released Dots, and Meta introduced Muse. Both AI agents use advanced AI models to work autonomously for tasks. They can even email or remind users when new messages arrive.

Apple's disclosure comes after several episodes that involved rogue AI agents breaking out of their sandboxes to hack third-party websites, like the Hugging Face incident, which has drawn renewed attention from policymakers in Washington, D.C., and around the world.

Separately, the ability of agents to use a computer in human ways is forcing big companies like Amazon and JPMorgan Chase to come up with policies around agentic access to services for strategic or security reasons.

Last month, Apple announced changes to full disk access on Macs to reduce "risks related to this level of access" for AI agents installed on PCs.

"The Company's ability to oversee and control third-party artificial intelligence agents and other technologies may be limited," Apple warned in its filing.

Apple doesn't currently have an agentic AI. But when new CEO John Ternus was asked on Monday's earnings call about AI agents, he said that the company had "real room" to expand Siri's capabilities.

This fall, Apple released Siri AI, a new assistant that uses the iPhone's local data storage to access data like calendars and emails, but Apple's Siri can't work autonomously for long stretches like Dots or Muse.

Ternus said Apple can't access private data on its devices, calling it a "core differentiator."

"If you think about it, so much of your personal data is on your iPhone, whether it's your contacts, your communications, health data, financial data, all of that," Ternus said. "Siri can tap into that and help you access it in a way that's very secure and private and that nobody else has access to."

Commercial AI agents like those from OpenAI and Meta ask for high-level access to the user's accounts like email in order to be more useful.

Apple indicated that the current regulations around AI and "super intelligence" are changing, unpredictable and that it could cost a lot of money to adapt to new laws governing AI.

"In addition, the Company is subject to the risks posed by ethical concerns and negative consumer perceptions related to automation, artificial intelligence and super intelligence, including perceived threats to safety or other societal harms or complications," according to the filing.

#2904. CrowdStrike's new kill switch can stop rogue AI agents in their tracks, CEO says

CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said Tuesday that the cybersecurity company can now shut down rogue artificial intelligence agents.

CrowdStrike's new kill switch, announced Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe conference, allows security teams to terminate misbehaving agents, prevent them from restarting and cut off their network access. It is designed to give humans more power over artificial intelligence models and systems, as concerns about how to control agents grow.

"Everyone is building AI agents, but they never had the off button, and now they do," Kurtz told CNBC's Jim Cramer on "Mad Money." "It's going to be an incredibly important part of making sure that AI agents run and operate safely."

The AI Kill Switch, as CrowdStrike calls it, is part of the company's Falcon Guardian platform, which monitors AI agents operating within an organization. The technology, which is available immediately, is aimed at addressing AI safety concerns surrounding the rapid adoption of AI agents.

In September, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei called for slowing AI development over safety concerns, a position backed by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. Kurtz argued at the time that slowing AI development would not eliminate existing security risks, which he said underscored the need for safeguards as companies deploy autonomous systems.

Kurtz said Tuesday that CrowdStrike's new kill switch represents a significant shift in cybersecurity, which has traditionally focused on protecting companies from external threats. As businesses rely more on AI agents, he argued that security providers must also ensure those systems operate safely within their own organizations.

"Enabling agents to work safely is a massive market opportunity for CrowdStrike," Kurtz said. "We have AI capabilities to prevent these breaches from happening on the outside, and we also have the capabilities to make sure that agents are basically doing what they are instructed to do."

The CEO compared the technology to airbags in cars, arguing that safeguards are necessary even when AI systems are designed to operate correctly.

"If they go outside of those guardrails at runtime, we can kill those agents," he said.

CrowdStrike is seeing growing demand for its security tools as businesses adopt AI. Kurtz pointed to CrowdStrike's partnership with Anthropic to protect its Claude Cowork product: He said enterprise customers had pushed for CrowdStrike's technology to be integrated before they deployed Anthropic's offerings.

"Our customers went to Anthropic and said, 'We need CrowdStrike before we can deploy AI,'" Kurtz said. "I think it says volumes that customers would not roll out AI without CrowdStrike."

Shares of CrowdStrike closed at a record high of $278.86 on Oct. 6 but have since fallen about 12% as tech stocks struggled in October and into November.

#2902. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2901. Why Luka Doncic is among great scorers — of all time

It's easy to overlook what Luka Dončić is doing.

He has been putting up video game numbers for so long that we're almost inured to them in the same way our olfactory senses stop responding to a constant smell.

But Giannis Antetokounmpo slapped us out of our slumber following Dončić's fourth 40-point performance in five days in the Lakers' 138-117 win over the Heat on Monday.

"Obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," Antetokounmpo said. "Not right now. Of all time. I feel like some people are scared to say that. But it's got to be out there."

Dončić has led the league in scoring twice and is on pace to do so a third time. He's averaging a blistering 38.3 points per game, 8.3 points more than second-place Stephen Curry. Here are the points Dončić has put up in the last five games: 42, 45, 37, 41, 52.

Yet somehow we've never thought of Dončić as an all-time scorer.

While Curry is the consensus greatest shooter ever, different players have been thrown into the conversation for all-time greatest scorer, including Michael Jordan, Wilt Chamberlain and even Kevin Durant, the latter of whom is a beacon of consistency but isn't going to regularly explode for 50-plus points.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles during a game against the Miami Heat at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

He has a career scoring average of 29.32 points, trailing only Jordan (30.12) and Chamberlain (30.07).

And Dončić is only 27 years old. He's still in his prime. He's only getting better.

Before JJ Redick became the Lakers' coach in June 2024, he said Dončić "has the best brakes in the fu–king world" on his former podcast, "The Old Man & The Three."

"What he does is there's a constant change of pace," Redick said. "He's on-balance. He's trying to get you off-balance. Once you're off-balance, he's still on-balance, he changes his pace."

In layman's terms: Dončić is impossible to guard.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic (77) drives past Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson in the second half of an NBA game in Los Angeles, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

Heat coach Erik Spoelstra knows that all too well. When asked about defending Dončić ahead of Monday's game, he pretty much intimated that it's a fool's errand.

"It's not going to be two defenders on him or even three," Spoelstra said. "You're pretty much going to go through your entire roster."

Dončić is an offensive wizard. He's a savant. He makes scoring look easy. And it's time for him to be considered among the all-time greats.

Against the Heat on Monday, Dončić had 24 points at halftime, becoming the first Laker to score at least 20 points in the first half in five straight games since Kobe Bryant in 2007.

He's averaging a historical 1.07 points per minute. The only other player who surpassed 1.0 was Chamberlain, who averaged 1.04 points per minute in 1961-62.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

And Dončić is scoring at an extremely efficient rate, averaging career highs in field-goal percentage (49.7) and 3-point percentage (42.7). He's also third in the league in assists with eight per game. Heck, he's even playing defense.

He's having one of the best starts in NBA history, becoming the third player to score at least 225 points through his first six games, alongside Jordan (1985-86) and Chamberlain, who did it twice in 1961-62 and 1962-63.

Jordan led the league in scoring 10 seasons and is widely considered the greatest player of all time. Chamberlain averaged 50.4 points per game during the 1961-62 season and is the only player to score 100 points in a game.

The greatest scorer of all time is not an easy debate to enter. But in the modern NBA, does anyone compare to Dončić? And historically, Dončić is already etching his name into the record books.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2900. Treasury yields fall after Democrats win House and Senate majorities

Treasury yields moved lower on Wednesday as investors weighed big wins from Democrats in the U.S. midterm elections.

The benchmark 10-year Treasury note yield fell 3 basis points to 4.917%. The 30-year bond yield slid 4 basis points to 5.289%, and the shorter-term 2-year note yield declined marginally to 4.606%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

Democrats obtained majorities in both the House and Senate. The party is projected to win at least 250 House seats and at least 54 in the Senate. Democrats flipped Senate seats from Republicans in Iowa, Kansas, North Carolina, Ohio, South Carolina, Maine and Texas. They also won key gubernatorial races in Ohio and Texas.

The resounding victory by Democrats could rein in government spending from the Trump administration, as it makes it harder for the White House to push legislation through Congress.

Traders at JPMorgan said one of the issues Wall Street is focusing on after the midterms is "reduced deficit spending."

"This can be achieved by asserting power over the budget and likely wading through a government shutdown," they said.

#2899. U.S. midterm elections in pictures: Democrats' sweeping victories deliver rebuke to Trump

Democrats scored sweeping victories in the U.S. midterm elections on Wednesday, flipping key Republican-held seats in Congress and securing historic wins in traditionally conservative states, as voters delivered a setback to President Donald Trump.

The party was projected to gain control of both chambers of Congress, with victories across several battleground states threatening to constrain Trump's legislative agenda for the remaining two years of his presidency.

Here are some of the scenes from polling stations, campaign headquarters and election-night celebrations across the United States.

Among the biggest surprises was Texas, where Democrat James Talarico defeated Republican Ken Paxton to become the state's first Democratic U.S. senator since 1988.

Fellow Democrat Gina Hinojosa also unseated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, becoming the state's first Latina governor and ending more than three decades of Republican control of the office.

In North Carolina, former Democratic Gov. Roy Cooper flipped a Republican-held Senate seat after defeating Trump-backed Michael Whatley. Cooper will succeed retiring Republican Sen. Thom Tillis, who announced his departure after clashing with Trump over his tax and spending legislation.

In Georgia, Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff secured reelection against Trump-backed Republican Mike Collins, while former Atlanta Mayor Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governor's race, becoming the first Black woman elected governor of a U.S. state.

Meanwhile, in Ohio, former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown staged a political comeback, defeating Republican incumbent Jon Husted in a closely watched Senate race.

The elections were widely viewed as a referendum on Trump's second term, with concerns over the cost of living, inflation and the war in Iran weighing on voter sentiment.

—CNBC's Danielle DeVries contributed to this article.

#2898. Buyers, sellers or fence-sitters? All 32 NFL teams slotted before the trade deadline

NFC East: Cowboys | Giants | Eagles | Commanders NFC North: Bears | Lions | Packers | Vikings NFC South: Falcons | Panthers | Saints | Buccaneers NFC West: Cardinals | Rams | 49ers | Seahawks

AFC East: Bills | Dolphins | Patriots | Jets AFC North: Ravens | Bengals | Browns | Steelers AFC South: Texans | Colts | Jaguars | Titans AFC West: Broncos | Chiefs | Raiders | Chargers

Where does your team sit as the NFL's trade deadline fast approaches?

Not all needy teams will swing trades prior to Nov. 10, but some certainly will look. On the other end of the spectrum, not all out-of-contention teams will hold fire sales, but with the recent spate of big names moving, there are opportunities to sell now and cash in later.

And, as we know, some teams will sit idly by as the deadline comes and goes. The NFL might be as active, trade-wise, as it has ever been in-season, but not every team is willing to pay the asking prices they're hearing.

Here's how we view all 32 teams, slotting each of them into one of three categories — buyers, fence-sitters and sellers — prior to the trade deadline. Teams are roughly ordered within each section by their perceived fits: strongest at the top, weakest at the bottom.

The Bills are the top seed in the AFC in spite of a defense that clearly needs help. Sunday's blowout of the Ravens notwithstanding, that unit isn't as likely to perform to that level against top-tiered quarterbacks and offenses. There are questions about how much the Bills want to give up in any prospective trade, and they're hamstrung a bit right now by salary-cap space (less than $4 million), but that can be rectified if needed. Safety might be the biggest position of need, although they also could look for extra pass-rush help.

Defensive tackle DaVon Hamilton suffered a torn quadriceps in Sunday's win over the Colts and will miss the rest of the season. It's hard to understate how big a blow this is. Head coach Liam Coen said the Jags will take a committee approach to replacing Hamilton, but finding a nose tackle is easier than finding other positions, so it would not be surprising to see them try to add a plugger up front. Safety is another spot they could consider. The Jaguars (5-2) are steamrolling toward a division title, and are in the hunt for the AFC's top seed. There's no better time to go for it.

They're already buyers after the DeForest Buckner trade, and they might not be done. They reportedly reached out to the Vikings about Jauan Jennings before they found out Jennings wasn't interested in a return. But receiver figures to be a position they consider upgrading, along with edge rusher and possibly cornerback. The 49ers have to be very cautious with Mike Evans; they've been a vastly better team with him on the field. Adding WR insurance, given all the injuries there, feels like a low-risk move that's on the table.

Their first buy of trade season, Jeffery Simmons, sunk his teeth into the Seahawks on Monday night. It's the kind of move that turned a very good team into a downright scary one. You can't blame the Bears if that's all general manager Ryan Poles gets done prior to the deadline, but the one area of concern coming out of the Seattle game had to be cornerback. Jaylon Johnson and Tyrique Stevenson both had rough nights with Malik Muhammad out, so we expect Poles to work the phones for CB help. They also could look for a pass-rush addition, with old friend Khalil Mack a name often bandied about these days. The Bears appear to be in Super Bowl mode now. They're out shopping.

The Vikings' offense could keep them from a Super Bowl, so they figure to seek help on that side of the ball — specifically a running back or an offensive guard. The run game has slowed down, and Aaron Jones Sr. has battled knee issues. The Vikings really could use more of a workhorse to complement Jones and Jordan Mason. They've also been connected to some pass rushers, scary as that sounds for opponents.

The Chiefs are stricken by limited salary cap space, but they figure to look and see what's out there. Wide receiver is the biggest area of concern — again — with Tyquan Thornton out for the season and Rashee Rice seemingly dealing with chronic soft-tissue issues. General manager Brett Veach has a decent history of smaller-to-medium deadline moves, so you can't rule anything out here. They also could mine for pass-rush help, but that might be tougher to find — and afford.

The primary need appears to be on defense, a unit that started strong but has tailed off. The Bengals could use help at multiple spots, although cornerback figures to be the most concerning, especially after watching Cam Ward pick them apart Sunday. A coverage linebacker and another pass rusher would be nice, too. On offense, if there's a need, it's at running back. They're averaging 3.7 yards per rush, with a long run of 15 yards this season.

Dallas is 3-5 yet still in the hunt for how poor the NFC East is. Team owner Jerry Jones says the Cowboys remain on the lookout for more help, and though they had been historically quiet at the trade deadline, Jones backed up his talk with last year's deadline deal for Quinnen Williams and the earlier-season trade for Joey Porter Jr. The Cowboys' defense is getting manhandled now. They badly need pass-rush help, but another corner would also be nice. Then again, so could more LB help. Jones said they won't want to flush more draft picks on a player who isn't under contract long term, but they might be desperate enough to add wherever they can.

The Patriots remain in contention at 5-3 (and a 3-0 mark in the division), but they could have concerns on both sides of the ball. On offense, they could use guard reinforcements after Alijah Vera-Tucker and Mike Onwenu landed on injured reserve. On defense, pass-rush help probably tops the list, but they also could seek LB and DB depth. Following the major A.J. Brown trade this summer, it would be surprising if the Patriots relinquished major draft assets. Any moves they make figure to be on the lower-compensation side, perhaps for a veteran's expiring contract.

Detroit fans have let general manager Brad Holmes know they want action prior to the deadline, especially after seeing the 49ers, Seahawks and Bears add starters last week. The Lions' defense is the biggest concern by far. The injuries to LBs Jack Campbell and Jimmy Rolder have left a big hole in the middle. Safety is in better shape with Brian Branch and Kerby Joseph back, but cornerback remains problematic. Pass-rush help also could be addressed. Can Holmes find the right help in the next week?

After adding Rashid Shaheed last year and Quenton Nelson this year, we know the Seahawks are open for business this time of year. Did Monday's home blowout loss to the Bears change their level of aggression? A struggling run game is an area the Seahawks could seek to help, although Zach Charbonnet's return could make them look elsewhere. They could try to add one more pass rusher and possibly another receiver for an offense that has been too JSN-dependent.

The Giants have found themselves atop the NFC East, and their first in-season trade for QB J.J. McCarthy has borne some early fruit. Could that move portend another (or others) if the Giants keep finding ways to win? Possibly. We know that John Harbaugh personally called Titans owner Amy Adams Strunk about Jeffery Simmons, but that call went to voicemail and Simmons went to Chicago. Defensive tackle figures to be the spot most likely to be addressed, but don't rule out a cheaper running back to stabilize that shakier position.

The Steelers already have been sellers, trading CB Joey Porter Jr. to the Cowboys. But sitting in first place in the AFC North in what's roundly expected to be Aaron Rodgers' final season, the Steelers might try to get back to buying. Not coincidentally, they could use a cornerback. Safety and wide receiver also could be spots they seek to upgrade. This doesn't feel like a team, however, that's prepared to go broke for that proverbial one final move.

They were close to being labeled "fence-sitters" after Sunday's breakout from RB Jonah Coleman. Running back had been the position that seemed most in need of being addressed prior to Coleman's big game Sunday. Now that feels like a luxury. Their most likely target position, if they try to swing a deal, figures to be offensive line or tight end.

This felt like an all-in season at one point, but that was before Myles Garrett and Davante Adams got hurt and the team started 2-4. The Rams have since evened their record at .500, but several tough games remain. The Rams certainly could be active, and another receiver certainly would help, but they've already depleted their trade assets significantly with the Garrett deal and don't have a ton to give up. They might want to add some help, but can the Rams realistically make an impact trade in time? It's unclear.

They've had some recent tailwind as a team, they've gotten Josh Jacobs back and Micah Parsons' return appears to be on the horizon. They also have some possible needs, including cornerback, if they can find the right trade partner. On the other hand, they've just been so historically quiet around the deadline under GM Brian Gutekunst (they actually have been more deadline sellers than anything), that I wonder if they'll do much.

The Texans have already bought, adding WR Marvin Harrison Jr., but is more coming? There are questions in the offensive and defensive backfields, and they continue to struggle to get the offensive line fully right. All those spots are possible. But the Texans are 2-5 and falling farther behind the first-place Jaguars. If Houston makes a move, expect it to be more on the minor end.

How seriously are the Browns taking their 5-3 start, especially after the humbling loss to first-place Pittsburgh? There are plenty of areas they could address, ample draft assets to part with and a general manager in Andrew Berry who has been among the most active in-season traders. But if the Browns don't feel this team has a legitimate chance to make playoff noise, they might be wise to keep their assets and load up more in the offseason. Browns fans would be giddy at a deadline deal, but patience might be preferred.

The Raiders feel like they are in no-man's land. They pulled back to .500 after losing four straight, but is Vegas capable of contending? The big drama now feels like when the Raiders will move off of Kirk Cousins and give the reins to Fernando Mendoza. There might be a case for the Raiders as sellers, with Cousins perhaps an intriguing piece for a team such as the Ravens that recently lost its starting QB. Other possible trade figures include Jackson Powers-Johnson and Adam Butler, but it also feels like the Raiders could ride things out without any major transactions the rest of the way.

The Panthers are a strange team to handicap. Carolina has clear needs, but at 3-4, with two straight losses, is it in position to add help? Oh, and did we mention that record puts the Panthers in first place? The playoffs are still very much a possibility, but this team being one or two moves away from being a Super Bowl contender is a stretch. If the Panthers add somewhere, cornerback feels like the most likely spot. Anything they do likely would have to be more than just a short-term rental at retail price.

Most of the NFC South profiles similarly to Carolina. There's a real shot at winning the division, but at what cost? There's also the chance the Bucs could look to sell, with names such as Yaya Diaby, A'Shawn Robinson and Chris Godwin Jr. being floated. But GM Jason Licht has not made an in-season trade since Tom Brady was QB, and it feels like the chances are greater that the Bucs do nothing.

They're 3-5 and 1-2 in the division, yet Atlanta has enough explosive potential offensively to be potential buyers. Even after Michael Penix Jr.'s four-INT game, the Falcons might think they have a real shot of taking the division. The receiver depth is light, and there are still holes to patch on defense, although they might treat the return of James Pearce Jr. from suspension as their big pre-deadline addition.

I'd put the Saints more in the "sellers" bin, sitting at 2-5 with team owner Gayle Benson already having aired her grievances with the team's performance publicly. A loss to the Browns this weekend might push them closer to moving some pieces. The question is about what players might be available and sought by other teams. If another team wanted Alvin Kamara or Cameron Jordan, the Saints would almost have to sell. Otherwise, there just aren't a lot of tradable assets that might tempt other teams.

The Jets certainly could be sellers, with the season again slipping away and the focus continuing to shift to what could be one of the franchise's biggest offseasons, armed with three first-round draft picks — with all three currently in the top 10. Adding to that should be a consideration. Garrett Wilson and Breece Hall are two big names that could be on the move, but to this point they've publicly pushed back against dealing either. If they are active, I expect lower-profile pieces such as Harrison Phillips or Nahshon Wright to be more likely than the bigger names.

The Commanders kept their season alive in the watered-down NFC East with a win over the Eagles on Sunday night, and there's a chance they could be lighter buyers if they can find a running back who fits their short- and long-term vision. They also could be sellers, with pass rusher K'Lavon Chaisson the most logical piece to try to move. It's hard to get a clear read on what GM Adam Peters wants to do, but he said he's talked to all 31 teams and continues to work the phones.

The Colts have already sold off two major assets in Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner. There's not much stopping them from continuing to put other players up for sale. WR Josh Downs has reportedly requested a trade and figures to be in play in the coming days. RB Jonathan Taylor might cost too much to be moved before the deadline, but the Colts are acting like a team just waiting for a bigger offer to pull the trigger. Lacking a first-round pick in 2027, the Colts need all the draft-pick help they can get.

For years, GM Howie Roseman has been about the most aggressive talent hunter out there come deadline time. This season, following a string of poor developments and six straight losses, the Eagles look far more like sellers on the outside. There is no shortage of players the Eagles could move. They have several expiring-contract candidates, including TE Dallas Goedert, CB Riq Woolen, WR Marquise Brown, DT Moro Ojomo and others. There also are players under contract beyond 2026 who could be available, including OG Landon Dickerson and Edge Nolan Smith. They've already made one trade and could be open for more business with this season apparently shot.

The Chargers have continued to hoard assets for now, but as the league's final winless team, it's worth wondering what they're waiting for. GM Joe Hortiz was turning away calls at one point because the team believed a turnaround was coming. It was not. If they finally become sellers, Khalil Mack figures to be an easy piece to move — a veteran pass rusher on an expiring deal. Teams seeking nose tackle help should probably call on Dalvin Tomlinson. WR Quentin Johnston also could be available if the team doesn't think he can reach his ceiling there. There are deals to be made if the Chargers shift their thinking.

The Dolphins are not making the playoffs this year, although it's reasonable to be encouraged by their competitiveness. Still, this is a sell-off phase for the Dolphins if they can cook up some interest in players such as Zach Sieler, Austin Jackson, Josh Uche or others. The problem is that Miami's roster is already so heavily loaded with rookies and minimum-salary players that there are only so many assets to move. But the Dolphins gladly will vacuum up any draft picks other teams are willing to fork over in this multiyear rebuild.

Shipping out well-liked Jeffery Simmons was a sign that the Titans are in sell-now mode. RB Tony Pollard is probably their most tradable piece, but it's not clear if they want to move him. Anyone can have WR Calvin Ridley for a song, if they want. Edge Jermaine Johnson II also might be a name you hear this week after picking him up from the Jets this offseason. This might not be a fire sale, per se, but they're apparently open for business.

Even after Sunday's dramatic win in Dallas, the Cardinals feel more like sellers than buyers, having already shipped out Marvin Harrison Jr. and sitting at fourth place in one of the toughest divisions. If a QB-needy team wants Jacoby Brissett, the team shouldn't hesitate trading him, especially after benching him for Gardner Minshew in the dramatic comeback. Pass rusher Josh Sweat feels like the biggest player who most reasonably could be moved now, although Budda Baker's name has also come up. They have some assets that could be moved, but will the Cardinals act on that?

In a single moment, when Lamar Jackson suffered his thumb injury against the Bills, the Ravens might have gone from buyers to sellers. The one exception might be at QB, where the Ravens could opt to swing a trade, be it for someone like Kirk Cousins or perhaps Bears backup Tyson Bagent, who played last year with Ravens OC Declan Doyle. But if the Ravens lose Thursday, they might consider moving a few players, including Marlon Humphrey, Mark Andrews and Roquan Smith.

#2897. Journalist says Juventus has been forced to focus on top four finish

Juventus are regarded as one of the biggest clubs in Italian football history, and expectations are always high whenever they begin a new season. Challenging for the Serie A title should be a regular ambition for the Bianconeri, given their history and financial strength.

The club consistently invests heavily in its squad to improve its chances of success, while the manager receives considerable backing to deliver results. However, Juventus have struggled this season and are no longer being seriously discussed as title contenders.

Their immediate concern is securing a place in the top four, especially after missing out on Champions League qualification last season. Another failure would represent a significant setback for the club and increase the pressure on the manager.

With the title race seemingly beyond their immediate reach, attention has turned towards securing a return to Europe's biggest club competition.

Journalist Gianni Balzarini believes the club's priorities have changed, with financial considerations now making Champions League qualification particularly important.

Returning to regular Champions League football would provide Juventus with an opportunity to restore some stability and generate the revenue needed to strengthen their position.

(Photo by Valerio Pennicino/Getty Images)

Discussing the club's situation, Balzarini explained the importance of finishing in the top four, via Tuttojuve:

"Now, for the club, winning a Scudetto isn't so important anymore; what matters is finishing in the top four, to get the Champions League revenue, and the proof is in the fact that this capital increase, which will be approved, responds precisely to this need. The club is 66 million down, eight more than last year, due to lost Champions League revenue. I mean, you always start from that base."

His comments highlight the financial consequences of missing out on Champions League football and explain why Juventus are prioritising qualification.

Although challenging for the Scudetto should remain a major ambition, the Bianconeri must first secure a return to Europe's elite competition.
