#2895. Google responds to political backlash over removing search from schools that ban AI

Google is playing defense on Tuesday after a political backlash to its new policy surrounding use of artificial intelligence in schools.

In a blog post titled "Designing responsible AI for learning," Google said it's bringing AI into its Classroom offering with a product called Guided Learning. The New York Times published a story saying that the company will turn off search for schools that reject AI entirely.

Following the Times' story, prominent left-leaning politicians, including Sen. Bernie Sanders from Vermont and New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani voiced their outrage on social media.

"Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools," Mamdani wrote on X. Sanders wrote that "Google is threatening schools," referring to the policy as "extortion."

Google responded later in the day. In a post on X, the company said its new policy was meant to give schools more choice over how students interact with AI. Google said its Gemini chatbot, Gemini Notebook and search are turned off by default, and that "schools are in control," with the ability to turn them as they see fit.

"We are also updating Search for schools to make it better for learning," Google wrote. "Specifically, AI Mode and AI Overviews will offer step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don't get quick answers to schoolwork."

The company's post didn't explicitly respond to the claim that access to search requires use of AI. However, Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

A Google spokesperson referred CNBC to the X post when asked for comment.

Google's broader practices have come under fire in recent years, with the company losing antitrust cases over its search and advertising businesses. Former Federal Trade Commission Chair Lina Khan aggressively pursued Google and other tech giants, including Meta and Amazon.

Khan said in a post on X on Tuesday that Google's new AI for learning policy highlights "the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service."

It's the latest controversy related to the impact of technology on children and the role leading internet companies are playing in delivering information.

In August, Meta reached a landmark $16.7 billion settlement in its child safety trial after a number of state attorneys general accused the company of misrepresenting the extent of child mental health harms caused by apps like Facebook and Instagram.

The same month, OpenAI launched ChatGPT for Teens after state attorneys general launched an investigation into the company's safety practices, with Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier, a Republican, filing a lawsuit in June.

The nonprofit Common Sense Media recently gave Google's AI overviews an "unacceptable risk" rating. Last month, Sens. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., and Josh Hawley, R-Mo., launched an inquiry over Google's push of AI into classrooms.

Google introduced AI overviews as a search function in May 2024.

— CNBC's MacKenzie Sigalos contributed to this report.

#2894. Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak

Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Chris Kuper was dealt a tough hand, but this is bad.

When the Eagles hired Kuper as their offensive line coach this offseason to replace the legendary Jeff Stoutland, the hope was that the Eagles would be healthy up front and return to dominance. That hasn't gone to plan.

And through eight games, the Eagles' struggling offensive line is one of the big reasons they are 2-6 and losers of six straight. But head coach Nick Sirianni still believes in Kuper. He gave an impassioned defense of his OL coach on Monday afternoon.

"Chris is coaching his [butt] off," Sirianni said on Monday. "He's fighting his [butt] off, he's coaching his [butt] off on a daily basis. You guys don't get to see that. I get it on a daily basis.

"You get to see that for three hours on Sunday, but he's coaching his [butt] off and he's fighting his [butt] off and I know he's giving the players good tools to use fundamentally and helping them be the best players that they possibly can be. But we all have to be better and we know that."

The next morning on 94WIP, Sirianni said more of the same about Kuper when asked about the way Stoutland departed the organization. He also said the Eagles' players have faith in Kuper and you can ask them. That might be true, but that can't matter more than results; and the results aren't good.

Through eight games, the Eagles are 30th in points and 32nd in total yards.

It's impossible to say if having Stoutland this season would have helped the Eagles greatly. But it's also impossible not to wonder, especially because his departure came after his role was diminished by Sirianni during the 2025 season. Stoutland is one of the greatest coaches in franchise history but even his line struggled last year.

It's also fair to point out that the line the Eagles have been using for much of the season is very different than what they expected entering the year. At the start of the season, the Eagles were banking on Lane Johnson, Landon Dickerson and Cam Jurgens. Well, Johnson retired after playing in three games, Jurgens was traded to the Ravens and Dickerson has played just 3 of 8 games.

Here's a look at the Eagles' OL throughout this season:

Week 1: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Cam Jurgens, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 2: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 3: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 4: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 5: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 6: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 7: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel BellWeek 8: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel Bell

In addition to the constant state of flux on the line, the Eagles have dealt with a ton of injuries to other players, including Jalen Hurts, DeVonta Smith, Saquon Barkley and more.

So it wasn't reasonable to expect the Eagles' offensive line to completely save them. But it's not unreasonable to expect better execution when it comes to things like assignments and communication. The Eagles have struggled with simple things up front at times this year. The Eagles have the 27th-ranked rushing offense in the NFL in 2026 and have given up a pressure rate of 42.65, which ranks 29th.

And the Eagles have been absolutely flummoxed by blitzes all year. They entered Week 8 allowing the highest pressure rate on blitzes in the NFL this season (57.7%), per NextGen Stats, and were even worse against the Commanders on Sunday Night Football. The Commanders generated 10 pressures on 12 blitzes (83.3%), which is the second-highest pressure rate on blitzes by a team in the 2026 season.

The Eagles have now faced 109 blitzes this season and have given up 66 pressures — an outrageous rate of 60.6% The Eagles have also allowed a free rusher on 18.2% of their passing plays this season, the most of any team, according to Ryan Paganetti.

A big focus for the offense this week is to fix the communication errors that have plagued them through eight weeks. Sean Mannion also expressed his confidence in Kuper this week.

"I think Koop's been doing an outstanding job," Mannion said. "I know, like I said, everything ultimately starts with me and making sure I'm charting the right course for us in terms of our protection plan, in terms of how we're getting it taught, in terms of our install meetings and everything like that. But Chris is doing a great job. He's a tremendous teacher of the fundamentals. He's great with those guys in the room."

Kuper was not retained by the Vikings after the 2025 season and the Eagles hired him with the hope that his knowledge in this system would help the transition to Mannion. Nothing on offense has worked this year.

So there's no doubt that the pressure on Kuper has started to get ratcheted up in recent weeks as the Eagles' struggles continue. But the Eagles seem like they're sticking with him for now.

There are a lot of problems with the Eagles' offense this season, as Sirianni is happy to share.

"Again, we all have to do better," he said. "I think, again, we want to zero in on one thing. We're not coaching well enough right now and we're not playing well enough right now. I know we're speaking offensively and right now we're not doing all the things we need to do to win football games, and that falls on everyone's shoulders. It's not just one thing. We have to dig deep as coaches, as players, and again, find solutions, find answers."

#2893. Dodgers’ All-Time Saves Leader Kenley Jansen Cast Off By New Team

The Los Angeles Dodgers have plenty of work ahead of them this offseason as they attempt to strengthen their roster and reclaim the World Series championship. After falling short of defending their title, the organization could look to make several significant additions before the 2027 campaign, with its bullpen once again a key area worth monitoring.

Eric Lauer, Kris Bubic, Brusdal Grateral, Evan Phillips, Blake Treinen and Alex Vesia are all free agents, giving the Dodgers some flexibility in reshaping a relief corps that appears less stable than the other roster units. And with the free agent and trade markets beginning to take shape ahead of the expected December lockout, the team has received an update involving one of its most accomplished former closers.

Phillies Infield Castoff Returns To Hated Division Rival After Cut

Kenley Jansen, the Dodgers' all-time saves leader who is approaching one of baseball's most exclusive career milestones, is suddenly available again after his latest team made a significant contract decision.

On Monday, the Detroit Tigers announced that they would not retain Jansen under his contract option for next season. The decision brings his one-year arrangement with Detroit to an end and gives the veteran closer another opportunity to find a new team.

"Kenley Jansen's quest to join the 500-saves club will have to continue with another team," Jason Beck wrote for MLB.com. "The Tigers declined their $12 million option on the legendary closer, paying a $2 million buyout and making the 39-year-old right-hander a free agent."

Jansen signed with Detroit last offseason after spending the 2025 campaign with the Los Angeles Angels. The move could put the Dodgers' former closer on the radar of organizations looking for experienced bullpen help, although there has been no indication that Los Angeles is pursuing a reunion.

Yankees' Former All-Star Gary Sanchez Declined By Brewers Shortly After World Series Win

Jansen established himself as one of the most accomplished relievers in Dodgers history after signing with the organization as an amateur free agent in 2004. He eventually converted from catcher to pitcher, becoming a fixture in Los Angeles' bullpen before departing after the 2021 season.

He recorded 350 saves with the Dodgers, comfortably establishing the franchise record ahead of second-place Eric Gagne's 161. Jansen was a key member of the 2020 World Series championship team that kicked off the ongoing golden age for the franchise.

"The future Hall of Famer enters his age-39 season with 498 career saves, third on the all-time list," Anthony Franco noted for MLB Trade Rumors. "While he's almost certainly not going to join Mariano Rivera and Trevor Hoffman in the 600 club, Jansen should celebrate his 500th save within the first month of next season."

Jansen finished the 2026 regular season with 22 saves and a 3.20 ERA across 45 innings.

Whether the Dodgers consider bringing back their former closer remains to be seen. But with Jansen now available and just two saves away from 500, his next destination will be worth watching as the offseason develops.

This article was originally published on Forbes.com

#2891. Giannis Antetokounmpo had very high praise for Luka Dončić after Lakers win over Heat

In what has become commonplace this season, even for his illustrious standards, Luka Dončić authored a magnificent performance during Monday night's 138-117 rout of the Miami Heat. The Slovenian superstar recorded 42 points (14-of-24 shooting), 13 assists (two turnovers), seven rebounds and two steals. After a couple relatively quiet games to begin the year, Dončić is averaging 43.4 points, 8.6 assists (3.6 turnovers), 8.0 rebounds and 2.0 steals over his past five outings.

"It feels good. Like I say, obviously when we win, it feels way better and that's the only important stuff," Dončić told reporters postgame. "But, obviously, it feels good. When you win by 20 against a team like Miami, it's really awesome, especially on a back to back."

While the 2-5 Heat are struggling out of the gates and the Toronto Raptors were without Kawhi Leonard, the Sunday-Monday back-to-back this week projected as one of Los Angeles' tougher early season tests when the schedule came out months ago. Of course, circumstances change but a pair of convincing wins (Sunday's late-game foibles aside) against two playoff hopefuls is a nice way for the Lakers to kick off November.

Following the Heat's loss, Giannis Antetokounmpo was asked by a reporter about how Dončić thrived as both a playmaker and scorer, given the fact defenses typically take away one of those aspects for a superstar while conceding the other. Antetokounmpo used the inquiry to bestow lofty praise upon Dončić, not just for his current league-wide standing but where he stacks up among the all-time greats.

"When you have a guy like Luka, obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," Antetokounmpo said.

When the reporter offered a "right now" caveat to Antetokounmpo's statement, the two-time MVP pushed back.

"Not right now, not right now. Of all time," he said. "I feel like some people are scared to say that but it's gotta be up there."

Dončić is on an absolute tear right now, leading the Lakers' second-ranked offense and playing some of the stingiest defense of his career. His next chance to keep the good times rolling comes Wednesday night on the road against the Sacramento Kings at 7 p.m.

#2890. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2889. Next Up: Coastal Carolina

Duke welcomes Coastal Carolina to Cameron on Thursday night, and that means seeing an old opponent back, because Coastal is coached by former Wake Forest guard Justin Gray.

Gray, who teamed up with Chris Paul, and who played for Skip Prosser, has done a really nice job so far at Coastal. His first season was 10-22, and last year, the Chanticleers finished 19-13. That doesn't sound imposing, but winning at the Sun Belt level isn't easy.

He also did well at Western Carolina in the Southern Conference, and that's not easy, either.

Gray has built his program around what he calls "the Coastal Edge," or just the Edge. For Gray, that means effort, toughness, and working hard together. Apparently, his practices are no joke, least of all on defense, so you can expect them to work hard on that end.

Coastal picked up 12 new players in the offseason, and Gray is going to push them hard on offense, too. He likes for his team to push the tempo and attack. Coastal spaces well, runs a lot of dribble-drives, and then kicks the ball out for threes.

It's not a high-level team, but if they get hot, Coast could be a very dangerous team. You don't want to let them get going.

Coastal's best player is arguably Trey Perry, who was part of that remarkable Miami of Ohio team from last year. He put up 20 points, 4 rebounds, and 3 assists in Coastal's 71-70 loss to Eastern Michigan.

We don't honestly know a whole lot about the rest of the roster, but sophomore Jaylen Cross played at Wake Forest last year, though he only averaged 4.9 minutes, and only got into 13 games.

There is another familiar name on the roster: Hardaway.

Penny's son, Ashton, started off at Memphis, left for St. Mary's, came back to Memphis, then departed his dad's program again, bound for Coastal.

Coastal has a reasonable shot at competing with Duke on the perimeter, but inside will be a major challenge. Gray has one player over 6-9, junior Caleb Middleton, who is 6-11, but who has one start in three seasons at three different schools.

Duke of course has several quality bigs, notably 6-11 Pat Ngongba and 7-2 JoJo Boumtje Boumtje, but Jon Scheyer has Drew Scharnowski, Cameron Williams, and Maxime Meyer in reserve.

He'd never do it, but we'd love it if at some point during the game, Scheyer put out Ngongba, Boumtje Boumtje and Williams, with Dame Sarr at the point.

Speaking of the perimeter, Sarr has significantly improved his offensive game. Cayden Boozer has become a steady, heady point guard, and may be a perfect fit. John Blackwell didn't have a good game against Army on Monday, but we've seen enough. He's really good.

Then there's freshman Deron Rippey, who had a jaw-dropping play against Army, a follow-up dunk that amazed everyone, including the Army defenders and Caleb Foster, who has built immense trust with the coaching staff.

Finally, there's no neat slot for Sebastian Wilkins and Bryson Howard. Both are essentially Swiss Army knives for Scheyer to deploy where they're needed, and both are likely to give Duke a huge boost when they get in the game.

We've learned not to get too cocky about supposedly lesser opponents, a lesson that Siena kindly reinforced last March. Gray is a quality coach who has earned respect. He'll be moving up soon.

Still, it's tough to see how Coastal overcomes Duke's size and versatility. It would surprise us if Scheyer ran out the big team we cheekily suggested above; it would not surprise us at all if he put out a small ball team with, say, Wilkins, Sarr, Rippey, Howard, and Boozer or Foster.

Because we won't be surprised if Gray's team puts significant early pressure on Duke that forces adjustments.

Do we think an upset is possible here? Well, it's always possible, but it seems unlikely. Still, we won't be at all surprised if Duke has a couple of anxious moments.

One last thing: no one has put Wake's Steve Forbes on the hot seat. For one, he's done about as well as he can with the resources Wake Forest gives him.

But if he does end up on the hot seat, it shouldn't surprise anyone if Gray's name enters the conversation.

#2888. Novo shares jump as Wegovy pill sales impress, drugmaker hikes outlook

Novo posted quarterly earnings ahead of expectations early Wednesday as sales of its Wegovy pill blew past estimates amid continued GLP-1 volume growth globally, sending shares up as much as 6% in early trading.

The company updated its outlook to now see adjusted revenue and operating profit growth between negative 2% and 2% at constant exchange rates. It previously saw full-year sales and operating profits, which it hiked in August, at negative 6% to flat for both metrics.

Sales in the third quarter came in at 79.2 billion Danish kroner ($11.9 billion), up 4% year-on-year at constant exchange rates and ahead of FactSet estimates of 73.6 billion kroner. Adjusted operating profit (EBIT) was 31.7 billion kroner, compared with the expected 28.6 billion kroner.

Novo saw "strong momentum" in the U.S., CEO Mike Doustdar told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday. "We today continue to see 80% market share with our product, despite the fact that competition has come and strengthened their presence," he said.

It comes as the Danish weight-loss drug maker is looking to regain momentum following a tumultuous year in which it fell behind rival Eli Lilly.

While Novo pioneered the GLP-1 weight-loss drugs now used by around 10% of Americans, it has struggled to keep the momentum going as chief rival Eli Lilly quickly caught up and surpassed Novo in their by far biggest market, the U.S. Several other pharma companies are also eyeing entry into the lucrative market.

Investors are now laser-focused on the pair's next-generation weight-loss drugs, and whether they can treat obesity-linked diseases such as heart and kidney conditions, sleep apnea and arthritis.

Novo is preparing to launch CagriSema, a combination of the GLP-1 receptor agonist semaglutide and the long-acting amylin analogue cagrilintide. Despite what Novo has touted as CagriSema's superior benefits over rival treatments, analysts have repeatedly questioned its commercial viability after the drug failed to beat Lilly's medicines that are already available.

Following the results, Citi analysts maintained their neutral rating on Novo stock, citing continued uncertainty over the near-, mid-, and long-term weight-loss drug pricing and a lack of a clear Wegovy successor in the pipeline.

Novo said sales of its Wegovy pill, launched earlier this year, totaled 5.4 billion kroner, compared with the roughly 4.5 billion kroner analysts expected.

Sales of injectable Wegovy and diabetes treatment Ozempic were 20.4 billion kroner and 29.7 billion kroner, respectively, also above expectations.

Novo also announced that Chief Financial Officer Karsten Munk Knudsen is leaving at the end of 2026 after 27 years at the company, including nine years as CFO. Luigi La Corte, currently CFO of Swiss dermatology company Galderma, will succeed him.

Novo's Wednesday earnings report comes after Lilly hiked its full-year outlook last week on the back of a better-than-expected third quarter, driven by strong demand for its diabetes and weight-loss injections Mounjaro and Zepbound.

Lilly's weight-loss pill Foundayo, however, missed analysts' expectations in the quarter even as the company's chief financial officer said total U.S. prescriptions and the number of new Foundayo patients doubled from the previous quarter.

Novo's Copenhagen-listed shares were last seen up 3.4%, bringing year-to-date losses to about 13%. The stock fell nearly 8% in one session in September after Novo failed to reassure investors with its post-Wegovy growth strategy.

Novo CEO Mike Doustdar told reporters early Wednesday that the company's recovery was gaining traction following successive upgrades to its full-year guidance, but cautioned that a turnaround could not be established on the basis of a single strong quarter.

"Turnaround and recovery is not done based on one data point, on one quarterly good result," Doustdar said, pointing to the progressive improvement in the company's outlook throughout the year.

"That gives me confidence that we're going in the right direction, and then we'll just have to continue working hard to see the recovery in full," he added.

#2885. Strand bookstore to pay nearly $1.5 million over Covid loan tied to Sen. Wyden's wife

The Strand, the iconic New York City bookstore, agreed to pay nearly $1.5 million in a civil settlement for accepting a Covid government loan that was off-limits because the store is owned by the wife of Oregon Democratic Sen. Ron Wyden, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office in Washington, D.C.

The Strand applied for the $932,350 loan in February 2021 from the Paycheck Protection Program administered by the Small Business Administration, U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro's office said Tuesday.

Store owner Nancy Bass Wyden certified on the loan application that "the spouse of a Member of Congress does not directly or indirectly hold a controlling interest in the applicant," Pirro's office said.

"Ms. Wyden's certification was knowingly and materially false and Strand was not eligible for the second draw loan it received," the office said.

The Strand will pay $1,482,560 to settle the case. There was no determination of liability by the store, Pirro's office said.

The Strand, in a statement, said the store made an "unintentional" mistake in applying for the loan, and said it was unaware at the time the funds were barred to businesses owned by the spouse of a member of Congress.

Bass Wyden's husband, who has served in the Senate for more than 30 years, is a staunch critic of President Donald Trump, who appointed Pirro to the post of top prosecutor in the District of Columbia.

Bass Wyden is the granddaughter of Ben Bass, founder of the Strand, whose flagship store is in Greenwich Village.

Congress authorized the forgivable PPP loans when it passed the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act in March 2020.

In December 2020, Congress provided additional funding for the PPP loan program with separate legislation, which "included a prohibition against businesses obtaining second draw PPP funds if a member of Congress or their spouse directly or indirectly held a controlling interest in the PPP loan applicant," Pirro's office said.

In a statement Tuesday, Pirro said, "The Paycheck Protection Program was created to save American jobs, not to enrich those who aren't eligible for taxpayer-funded relief, especially the wife of a U.S. Senator."

Her office noted that the False Claims Act, which the Strand was alleged to have violated, "allows the government to establish liability based on reckless disregard and permits the government to recover more than its actual losses," which is what occurred in this case.

The civil settlement resolved claims brought under a so-called qui tam lawsuit, which allows private whistleblowers to sue on behalf of the U.S. government and receive a portion of any recovery.

The qui tam action in this case was filed by a corporate entity, Blockquote Inc., in U.S. District Court in Washington. Blockquote, whose owners were not disclosed by Pirro's office, will receive a total share of about $148,256 in connection with the settlement.

The docket for Blockquote's lawsuit is sealed from public view.

The Strand, in a statement after CNBC requested comment from the store and Bass Wyden, said the restriction that the store violated was passed by Congress between the first and second rounds of the PPP.

"Unfortunately, the bookstore's accountant and the bank administering the loan were not aware of this change when the second-draw application was processed," the Strand said.

"We stand by the facts: the circumstances arose during an unprecedented and rapidly changing public health emergency, the mistake was unintentional," the store said. "We hope this matter will be viewed objectively and in its proper context, with the understanding that the application of justice should always be fair, impartial, and free from political considerations," the store said.

A spokesman for Sen. Wyden's office said the senator and his wife do not discuss her business, and that he had no involvement in the loan or settlement.

#2884. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2883. China expands overseas mining stakes, tightening grip on critical minerals

China is mobilizing state-linked companies to take bigger ownership stakes in mines overseas, seeking to strengthen its stranglehold over the metals vital for electric vehicles, semiconductors and jet engines

The state-led push could give Beijing greater leverage over global supplies of critical minerals and who gets access to them, analysts say.

"China is rolling out a state-coordinated global mining strategy" to replace firm-level decision-making, said Dan Wang, China director at Eurasia, adding that the model would allow for larger, better-aligned global mining investments.

In April, a dedicated overseas bureau was set up to oversee investments abroad by central state-owned enterprises. Several government agencies, ministries and policy banks have been mobilized to steer overseas mining deals.

Later in July, a mining investment vehicle was created to coordinate overseas deals, advising Chinese companies on compliance, risk management, and market conditions as they face growing geopolitical tensions and regulatory hurdles abroad.

The push for greater centralization could curb disorderly competition among Chinese firms and strengthen their hand against Western competitors and newer entrants, such as Saudi Arabia and India, Wang said, though a longer review and approval process could also raise corruption risks and weigh on investment returns.

Chinese firms have invested heavily in overseas mining for two decades, making big bets in places avoided by Western miners, from Tajikistan to the Democratic Republic of Congo. The push has gained urgency as governments increasingly view critical minerals as a source of geopolitical leverage.

Chinese mineral acquisitions abroad reached $8.8 billion in the first three quarters this year — already the highest annual total since at least 2018, according to a Rhodium Group report released Wednesday.

"Acquisitions have mostly been concentrated in minerals that China lacks domestically," said Armand Meyer, senior research analyst at Rhodium, naming lithium, nickel and cobalt during the electric-vehicle boom in the mid-2010s and gold in recent years.

Outbound investment in upstream critical minerals will likely roughly double from the 2021-2025 period, according to Eurasia, as China doubles down on overseas push to secure access and reserves that it lacks domestically.

Beijing treats 70% import dependence as an implicit red line – which puts copper, lithium, cobalt, nickel, bauxite and iron ore under a hard national security constraint to expand supply abroad, Wang said.

Even where reliance is lower, Beijing wants more. For gold, which sits within the 50% "safe" range, the government still seeks additional access to high-grade resources, Wang said. That pursuit of scale and "ultimately geopolitical strength" could override decisions over investment risk and profitability, she added.

But the push is facing growing resistance as host governments demand more local processing, a stage China dominates and has turned into a key supply-chain chokepoint. More resource-rich countries are restricting exports of unprocessed minerals and requiring foreign partners to refine them locally.

Countries have long shipped rare earths to China for processing because refining carries high environmental costs, allowing Beijing to build out massive processing capacity across the country. The U.S. has abundant supplies of rare earths, but it relies on China to refine them.

China now produces about 92% of the world's refined rare earths, according to the International Energy Agency.

That gives Beijing the ability to withhold supply or flood the market to influence prices and project power, said Brad Handler, director of Energy Finance Lab at the Payne Institute. "If a rival builds a new refinery, China can produce more, oversupply the market and push prices below economic levels," he said.

China has curbed exports of seven medium and heavy rare earths since last year, requiring exporters to obtain licenses.

Japan has been among the hardest hit, reportedly coming within months of running out of yttrium. The element, used for screen displays and semiconductors, is produced as a byproduct during the extraction of other rare earth metals.

China's exports of yttrium oxide to Japan collapsed to 4,000 kilograms in September from 51,000 kilograms a year earlier, according to Chinese customs data. And the oxide's price reportedly surged to about $1,150 a kilogram outside China — roughly 179 times its level two years earlier — compared with about $9 a kilogram inside the country.

Japan spent 16 years building stockpiles after China's 2010 restrictions, yet "there is virtually no yttrium or lutetium separation capacity outside of China," Bedford said.

But Chinese companies have adapted in recent years, ramping up greenfield investment and setting up processing capacity near the mines, instead of outright acquisitions, according to Rhodium.

"That is most visible where host governments require local processing," said Meyer, pointing to Indonesia's ban on nickel ore exports that has led Chinese firms to build smelting and refining capacity in the Southeast Asian country, instead of shipping them back to China.

#2881. Jaguars' defense aiming for 25th straight game not allowing a 75-yard rusher

With the Ravens down to their third-string quarterback on Thursday night against the Jaguars, Baltimore will rely heavily on running back Derrick Henry. Unfortunately for the Ravens, it's been a long time since a running back has had a big game against the Jaguars' defense.

The Jaguars haven't allowed an opposing runner to gain 75 or more yards in a game in the last 24 games. If the Jaguars don't allow a 75-yard runner against the Ravens on Thursday night, they will become just the third team in the Super Bowl era to go 25 straight games without allowing an opposing runner to gain 75 yards.

Henry is leading the NFL with 158 rushing attempts this season, and he may very well get the ball even more than 20 times per game he's averaging so far this season. Undrafted rookie quarterback Joe Fagnano is making the first start of his career for the Ravens, so they're likely to need to get a big game running the ball if they're going to pull off the upset over the Jaguars.

Although Henry is still carrying the ball a lot at the age of 32, his effectiveness has waned. He's averaging a career-low 4.0 yards per carry. It won't be easy to have a breakout game against the Jaguars, but the Ravens need him to do it.

#2880. Fun, flowing, not yet flawless: Lakers' league-best offense still has room to improve

Austin Reaves described it as "fun." Luka Doncic called the Lakers' effortless-looking offense as "flowing." And this is still the early stages of development for a team that brought in nine new players.

Through eight games, the Lakers have the most dynamic offense in the NBA. Led by Doncic, the Lakers are second in scoring with 122.5 points per game and first in offensive rating with 121.9 points per 100 possessions. The starting lineup of Doncic, Reaves, Walker Kessler, Ziaire Williams and Matisse Thybulle has a league-best 135.5 offensive rating among five-man groups that have played at least 40 minutes together entering Wednesday's game at Sacramento.

"Everything's as expected," guard Quentin Grimes said Monday after the Lakers hung 138 points in a 21-point win over the Miami Heat. "Winning, everybody's getting involved, everybody's making the right play, getting a lot of assists."

The Lakers (6-2) had 39 assists against the Heat, the most during JJ Redick's head-coaching tenure. Doncic is on a historic scoring binge; he is the first Laker since Kobe Bryant in 2007 to have five consecutive games of 35 or more points. While putting up a seemingly ho-hum 42 points against Miami, he also had a season-high 13 assists.

"It's a really good offense that we're playing right now," Redick said. "And a lot of that, of course, is Doncic and what he's able to do and bend the defense."

Doncic, the NBA's leading scorer at 38.3 points per game, does his damage early. He typically plays all 12 minutes of the opening quarter and leads the league with 15 points a game in the first quarter. Once he gets his shot going, teams send double teams to force the ball out of his hands. That's when Doncic can be particularly dangerous.

Read more: Luka Doncic's 42-point effort carries Lakers past Giannis Antetokounmpo and Heat

Doncic has always been lauded for his superior court vision. But the Lakers are still learning to see the same picture. The Lakers had 14 turnovers, including 11 from Doncic and Reaves, during a preseason game against the Denver Nuggets, who were aggressively trapping the Lakers' ballhandlers. The Lakers threw away a 25-point second-quarter lead on Oct. 30 when the Golden State Warriors ratcheted up the ball pressure.

Doncic's five turnovers a game are the second most in the league, and Reaves is third with 4.6 turnovers per game.

Some players are new to being on a team with guards who get blitzed nearly every play, Reaves said. It requires players to not only be comfortable making decisions to pass, drive or shoot, but they have to choose quickly. A moment's hesitation gives away the four-on-three advantage created by the double-team.

Redick praised Grimes' ability to keep advantages by driving decisively. Forward Sandro Mamukelashvili is also great at it, Redick said, as is guard Collin Sexton, who knows no speed other than full. The Lakers have also finished their advantages with Thybulle and Williams making27 of 58 (46.6%) three-point attempts combined.

"That's the beauty of our team," Williams said. "You could put different guys at different spots and everyone's able to make a play. All our guys are just dynamic and versatile. And everyone just wants to make the right play. No one wants to only shoot, there's no egos. And that's the fun part about it, you know? We all love to see each other succeed."

Williams missed three games because of a sore left foot but returned Monday with nine points, two rebounds and two assists off the bench. When Jake LaRavia jumped into the starting lineup, the Lakers were just as effective offensively as their first-choice lineup. When Doncic, Reaves, Kessler, Thybulle and LaRavia are together, the Lakers' 144.3 points per 100 possessions ranks first among lineups that have played at least 30 minutes.

Heat forward Giannis Antetokounmpo reaches through the hoop to block a reverse dunk by Lakers center Walker Kessler on Monday night. (Robert Gauthier / Los Angeles Times)

But numbers don't normalize until about 250 minutes, Redick believes.

"I think there's always room for improvement," he said Monday.

The Lakers have the most room for improvement on the defensive side. Their defensive rating of 114 points allowed per 100 possessions is tied with the Clippers for 20th in the NBA. They still rank 28th in rebounding, despite three consecutive double-doubles from Kessler, who responded after Redick challenged the center to be a larger rebounding presence.

"This team can become a great team," Redick said before Monday's win over the Heat. "That requires patience, that requires a lot of coaching and teaching and buy-in, and so that whole process takes time."

#2879. Mortgage rates drop for the third straight week, but homebuyers are still leaving the market

After a brief bounce the week before, mortgage demand continued its slide last week, despite falling mortgage rates. Total application volume dropped 1.2% week to week, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association's seasonally adjusted index.

The average contract interest rate for 30-year fixed-rate mortgages with conforming loan balances, $832,750 or less, decreased last week to 7.3% from 7.37%, with points declining to 0.75 from 0.76, including the origination fee, for loans with a 20% down payment.

"Mortgage rates drifted down to their lowest level since September 2026 last week in the wake of the Federal Reserve's decision to hold on additional rate hikes at its October meeting," said Mike Fratantoni, senior vice president and chief economist at the MBA.

Refinance demand was essentially flat for the week, rising just 0.1%. It was, however, 60% lower than the same week one year ago. Mortgage rates, despite falling for now three straight weeks, are still about a full percentage point higher than they were at this time last year. That gives borrowers very little incentive to refinance.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

Applications for a mortgage to purchase a home fell 2% from the previous week and were 19% lower year over year. The housing market shows no signs of thawing, after the sharp rate increase in September sent it into a deep freeze.

The share of adjustable-rate mortgages, which offer lower rates but carry the risk of eventually resetting higher, continued to climb. The average rate on a 5/1 ARM dropped to 6.29% from 6.41% the previous week.

"As we have highlighted in recent weeks, more borrowers are choosing ARMs in this environment to lower their initial payments, with the ARM share at its highest level since September 2025," added Fratantoni.

Mortgage rates moved slightly higher to start this week, according to a separate survey by Mortgage News Daily. That came after a hotter-than-expected report on job openings.

"All else equal, stronger labor market data tends to put upward pressure on rates. Tech stocks were also modestly higher [Tuesday], and weakness in the tech sector has been a key factor driving persistently lower rates over the past several weeks," wrote Matthew Graham, chief operating officer at Mortgage News Daily.

Correction: Total application volume dropped 1.2% week to week, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association's seasonally adjusted index. An earlier version misstated the figure.

In June, both chambers — which were then Republican controlled — passed a concurrent resolution to end the conflict, although a concurrent resolution is not binding and does not have the force of law. Previous attempts to impose a binding War Powers Resolution had been defeated.

After Lions linebacker Jack Campbell was taken off the field on a backboard early in the fourth quarter against the Vikings and transported to a local hospital on Sunday, Nov. 1, the team said he was being evaluated for a neck injury. They have provided few specific medical updates since, citing respect for Campbell's family. After being off Tuesday, Lions players return to practice Wednesday, Nov. 4. Head coach Dan Campbell is scheduled to have a press conference at 1 p.m.

Here is what the Lions and Dan Campbell have said about Jack Campbell's injury:

➤ In a postgame radio interview with Lions Radio Network's Brad Galli, Dan Campbell said Jack Campbell was alert and breathing on his way off the field.

➤ In his postgame press conference, Campbell said: "Thoughts and prayers are with Jack and his family. He's being taken care of. We don't have any new information. We'll keep you updated as this goes, but we're thinking about him."

➤ At 7:08 p.m. on Sunday night, the team released this statement: "Jack Campbell is resting and will remain in a local hospital tonight, for continued evaluation and monitoring. The team will provide further updates as we have them."

➤ At 2:31 p.m. on Monday, the Lions announced: "Jack Campbell continues to rest and recover at a local hospital. Preliminary indications are positive and out of respect for Jack and his family we will not share further information at this time."

➤ At his press conference about half an hour later, Dan Campbell referred to the team's statement and said, "It's positive. So your prayers are working. Please continue to do that and we'll leave it at that. So, just continue to pray for him, but it is positive news on Jack."

➤ Asked if he could say any more on Jack Campbell's injury, the coach said, "I can't, so I'm not going to." He added, "The team knows what's going on. I've given it to the captains."

➤ At his Monday press conference, Dan Campbell also said he visited Jack Campbell and his family at the hospital on Sunday night: "It was good. It was positive. Got to see his parents, talk to his wife. And everybody's in good spirits. And it's about the recovery and it's a small setback, that's the family. They're very positive."

➤ On "Costa & Jansen with Heather" radio show on Tuesday, Dan Campbell said of Jack Campbell, "The good news is he is improving." Dan Campbell added that he's kept the team captains updated on the linebacker's status and that the idea that he is "doing better" is "good news" for the players who are concerned about his health.

#2875. AI data center stocks and Musk's SpaceX decline as Texas governor result raises risks of a slowdown

AI data center stocks came under pressure as Democrats — seen as proponents of moratoriums on the buildout — won big in midterm elections and in key technology hubs like Texas.

Markets were already anticipating the Democrats to win the House of Representatives and possibly take Senate control. But the Texas governor mansion going to Democrats was a big blow, analysts said. Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who wants to enact an immediate moratorium on data centers in the state, won over incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott. According to Wells Fargo, 27% of planned data center gigawatts is in the Lone Star State.

The midterm elections will slow the pace of data center buildouts across the U.S., Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"There is no way to sugarcoat that Democrats taking control especially over the Senate will create some major hurdles for AI and tech legislation in the Beltway over the next 2 years," he said.

He added that the election in Texas was the "biggest worry" of the night for tech investors and the AI trade because that's where around 25%-30% of data center gigawatts and capacity will come from.

Shares of Elon Musk's SpaceX shed 7%. The Democrats' victory in Texas is a big setback for Musk, where he heavily backed Republican candidates, and where his companies Tesla and SpaceX are based. Musk spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms and funneled $224 million into Texas alone, where Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott lost to Democrats.

SpaceX is currently building out multiple gigawatts of data center capacity, including projects in Texas.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk said in a post on X in the early hours on Wednesday, referring to his speculative plans to build AI infrastructure off-planet.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Iren fell by 7% and 6%, respectively. Peer Nebius Group was down 3% and Oracle shed 4.3%. Nvidia shares were slightly in the red.

Tech giants Alphabet, Meta and Microsoft mostly bucked the trend and were higher.

The Democratic win in Texas adds to existing pressure on the bull-market-leading AI trade. The Nasdaq-100 index fell into correction territory to end October as concerns mounted that the growth of data center spending by big hyperscalers and private AI juggernauts OpenAI and Anthropic was slowing.

"We believe the equity market reaction from the midterms mostly depends on the outcome of the Texas governor's race — a Dem win could potentially be a left-tail event to the AI trade, warned Ohsung Kwon, Wells Fargo's chief equity strategist, in a report this week.

Democrats won or retained the governorships in other key data center states Georgia, Pennsylvania and Ohio. Georgia represents 7% of all the planned U.S. datacenter gigawatts, while Ohio and Pennsylvania account for 5% apiece, estimates Wells Fargo.

Notable investment strategist Michael Hartnett of Bank of America warned earlier this year that Democrats winning the Senate, combined with taking the Texas governorship could lead to a "big slump" in stocks of more than 10% into year-end.

"Data centers went from being a popular economic development tool in 2024 and 2025 to something of a pariah by the time the midterm campaigns gathered steam," wrote the Washington policy team at Optimal Advisory this week. "The midterms are likely to send a message to local and state officials that there is a political upside to blocking, delaying and/or raising the cost of data centers."

Savannah James attends the Blue Jacket Fashion Show, an event supporting prostate cancer awareness and men's health through fashion and philanthropy, in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Sept. 30, 2026. BFA.com / BACKGRID

Savannah described New York as the primary meeting point between her and LeBron, who decided to live in the Big Apple while playing for the 76ers.

"So, New York will be the hub. So it's LA, New York and Philly. And my son is in school in Tucson, Arizona. Trying to plan my life out like that is a little crazy for me right now," she said.

Savannah, who turned 40 years old this summer, spent a lot of time reflecting on entering a new decade and how she wanted to approach anything that life threw at her.

Savannah James absolutely stunned on the red carpet at the TIME100 Sports Gala. FilmMagic

"LeBron going to play in Philly, it's going to be an adjustment, but it is going to work out. Zhuri going into middle school is an adjustment, but it's going to work out. And then the boys are kind of living their own lives.

"But I think the way that it feels for me is that I feel different in myself, in the work that I've done and the way that I wanted to feel going into this milestone age.

"I am happy to be in this space right now; it feels good."

The James family may not reside together anymore, but Savannah appears determined to be the glue of the group.

#2873. Private companies added 86,000 jobs in October, slightly higher than expected, ADP says

Private sector job growth held steady in October, with gains spread across sectors, as the labor market showed fresh signs of stabilization, ADP reported Wednesday.

The payrolls processing firm said companies added 86,000 jobs during the month, the same as the downwardly revised total for September and slightly ahead of the Dow Jones estimate for 75,000.

Employment gains came mainly in the service sector.

Trade, transportation and utilities led with 39,000 new jobs, while education and health services added 28,000, and leisure and hospitality contributed 19,000. Other sectors showing gains included financial activities (15,000), construction (13,000) and other services (10,000).

However, some of the gains were offset by a decline of 27,000 in professional and business services and a loss of 8,000 in natural resources and mining. Information services, a sector potentially impacted by the artificial intelligence buildout, lost 2,000 jobs.

Salaries continued to show gains, particularly for those changing jobs — those workers saw a 4.8% annual gain. A report Tuesday from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed that the number of people quitting their job, a sign of labor market mobility, hit its highest level since March 2025.

Workers overall saw a 3.2% base pay gain, while gross pay increased 4.7% annually. Pay for those staying in their jobs rose 3%.

"Employers of all sizes were hiring last month, and our data suggests that job-changers were lured by a combination of bonuses, overtime, and more hours in addition to higher base pay," said ADP's chief economist, Nela Richardson. "Taken together, earnings growth for these workers hit its fastest pace since March 2024."

Companies with between 50 and 499 workers added 33,000 jobs. Large businesses added 24,000, while small firms increased rolls by 28,000.

The ADP report serves as a precursor to the BLS nonfarm payrolls count to be released Friday.

Economists surveyed by Dow Jones are looking for the BLS report for October to show a gain of 59,000 after September's modest growth of 29,000.

#2872. Reid Keeps Apologizing For Chris Jones’ Lack Of Production

The Kansas City Chiefs have a pass rush that isn't getting home to produce sacks, and it's becoming abundantly clear that defensive tackle Chris Jones is at the heart of the problem.

The Chiefs are paying Jones a huge amount of money to produce sacks or help his teammates get them, and he's been shut out on the stat line in two straight games now, per a report from Myles Simmons of Pro Football Talk.

Those two games are losses, and that's not a coincidence, although Simmons left that part up in the air in his piece. Jones has just 13 total tackles with one tackle for loss, three QB hits, and a half-sack through seven games in 2026, and you can make a credible argument that he's the most overpaid player in the NFL.

Coach Andy Reid doesn't agree, despite the fact that the Chiefs have just eight sacks this season, which is just over one per game. Per Simmons, only the Philadelphia Eagles have a lower sack rate than Kansas City, which has been getting one on just 3.69 percent of opponent dropbacks. By contrast, Minnesota leads the league with a 12.32 percent sack rate, with Chicago at No. 2 at 9.65 percent.

The coach is known for backing his veterans during tough times, and he continues to do that with Jones. His latest version of this has Jones "giving his all" and "doing everything he can do," as he continues to focus on the double teams Jones consistently draws.

The technical explanation Reid offered had to do with rushing four lineman and trying to get home, and he threw defensive line coach Joe Cullen under the bus for being the architect of that approach.

Reid also cited the number of blocked balls as a sign that the Chiefs are "getting close," but this isn't horseshoes or hand grenades. The coach also included a comment on the trajectory of the ball being a factor in the lack of sacks, which took the art of excuse-making to a new level.

The word salad part had the Chiefs wanting to not just get "to" the quarterback, but "on" the quarterback. The semantic hair-splitting can't hide the numbers, and the glare of the spotlight on Jones is increasing with each passing week.

Fortunately for the Chiefs, the schedule could provide a temporary answer, as they play the New York Jets, who rank 26th in the league in allowing sacks, per Simmons.

#2870. Uber beats on revenue even as mobility and delivery units miss estimates

Uber reported revenue for the third quarter that topped analysts' estimates, but the company missed expectations in its two key businesses.

Here's how the company did versus analysts' estimates compiled by LSEG:

Revenue increased 10% from $13.47 billion a year earlier. The company reported net income of $512 million, down 92% from a year earlier, when the company had a $4.9 billion benefit from a tax valuation release.

Uber's core mobility unit, which is its ride-hailing service, saw a 2% decline in revenue to $7.52 billion, missing the average estimate of $7.71 billion, according to StreetAccount. Revenue in the food delivery business increased 23% to $5.49 billion, trailing the $5.51 billion average estimate.

Total bookings of $59.82 billion were about in line with the $59.83 billion average analyst estimate, according to StreetAccount.

Uber's revenue beat was due to growth in its freight division. Sales jumped 34% to $1.76 billion, topping the StreetAccount consensus of $1.49 billion,

Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi said, in prepared remarks ahead of a scheduled call, that the company's core mobility segment performed slightly below expectations overseas.

"Elevated fuel prices and tighter driver availability weighed on supply growth in several international markets," he said, adding that Uber is making "targeted investments to strengthen driver supply."

The company plans to have over 2,000 automated vehicles available on its platform by the end of 2027, half operating in the U.S. and half internationally. It expects to have around 700 available on its platform by the end of the year, adding Amazon's Zoox and Motional robotaxis to its platform in Las Vegas this quarter.

Uber is also growing its delivery business through acquisitions of Delivery Hero in Germany and ezCater in the U.S.

In its Uber Eats business, a recently expanded partnership with Costco in the U.S. is "tracking ahead of expectations," Khosrowshahi said. The company said Costco is now one of its top merchants in terms of gross bookings.

For the fourth quarter, Uber sees overall bookings of $64.5 billion at the mid-point of its forecast, in line with the average StreetAccount estimate of $64.26 billion.

#2868. SpaceX signs deal with Qualcomm to provide network technology for U.S. mobile service

SpaceX announced a deal with chipmaker Qualcomm to provide key network technology for Elon Musk's Starlink Mobile service, a challenger to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile.

Qualcomm said on Wednesday that the terrestrial network it will help SpaceX build will "complement Starlink's satellite capabilities," with a planned rollout to begin next year. Financial terms of the multiyear deal weren't disclosed.

"Together with SpaceX, we are building world-leading cellular infrastructure in the United States, advancing 5G today, with an opportunity to lay the foundation for AI-native 6G in the years ahead," Durga Malladi, an executive vice president at Qualcomm, said in the statement.

Starlink is a critical piece of SpaceX's business, accounting for 36% of the company's revenue in the third quarter and 85% of its operating profit. But to date, the company's consumer business has been limited when it comes to mobile direct-to-device services as its satellite network can't provide all the voice, video and data coverage that competitors offer on popular smartphones.

Now SpaceX is snapping up spectrum and forging partnerships to take on the major mobile carriers. Last month, the company announced an agreement to purchase a nationwide spectrum portfolio in the 800 MHz band. The news sent shares of U.S. carriers reeling. Earlier this week, the Washington Post reported that SpaceX is in talks to acquire EchoStar's Boost Mobile.

SpaceX went public in June in the largest IPO on record and is now valued at close to $2 trillion.

With Qualcomm, SpaceX will be using the chipmaker's Dragonwing cellular infrastructure hardware and software in base stations to enable high-capacity coverage for Starlink Mobile.

Qualcomm has a long history of making chips for cellular networks, but is best known for making the modems and radios that go inside devices, not base stations for running a wireless network. The company has targeted carriers and other infrastructure companies since it made a bigger push into the 5G infrastructure market in 2020.

SpaceX represents one of Qualcomm's highest-profile customers or partners in 5G infrastructure, following earlier deals with Verizon, Dish, and Europe's Vodafone.

While SpaceX plans to use Qualcomm's technology to establish tens of thousands of new cell sites, it hasn't announced partners among cell tower companies or said how many new towers may be required.

Mobile carriers generally lease space from companies such as American Tower and Crown Castle, which commonly host multiple carriers from one site.

SpaceX did not respond to CNBC's request for additional information.

-- CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed reporting.

#2867. Cops complain about LeBron James during J.R. Smith assault arrest: ‘I’m still mad’

Cops complained to J.R. Smith about LeBron James just seconds before they arrested the former New York Knicks star earlier this year, according to new police video obtained by the California Post.

Smith was stopped by Royse City Police Department officers on a Texas roadway on Sept. 28 after they suspected he had a warrant out for his arrest.

Bodycamera video shows J.R. Smith talking with police officers in Texas. ROYSE CITY PD

The cops asked him to exit his vehicle while they investigated the situation, and as they were trying to get the matter squared away, they struck up a conversation about basketball.

Smith told them he was a former NBA player who retired in 2020. He also explained how he broke into the league and what teams he played for.

Then one of the officers asked him if he had a championship on his résumé.

Smith went on to explain he got the rings during his time on the Cleveland Cavaliers and the Los Angeles Lakers.

J.R. Smith chats with police officers about the Lakers in September. ROYSE CITY PD

"So, you got a ring with Kyrie [Irving]," one of the cops asked.

Cops stopped J.R. Smith after they suspected he had a warrant out for his arrest. ROYSE CITY PD

The cop, who appeared to be a Dallas Mavericks fan, then made it clear he was not James' biggest supporter, as he called the former Lakers superstar, "He who shall not be named."

"He stole Luka [Doncic] from us," the officer said, referencing the historic Lakers-Mavericks trade that sent Doncic to LA in February 2025. "He stole Luka from us. That was all him."

JR Smith of the Los Angeles Lakers drives to the basket against the Toronto Raptors on Saturday, Aug. 1, 2020, at The Arena in Orlando, Fla. NBAE via Getty Images

"Nobody was happy about that," one of the other cops on the scene added.

"I'm still mad," the other officer said. "Still mad."

Just moments later, Smith was placed in handcuffs after it was confirmed he had an active warrant.

Los Angeles Lakers forward LeBron James controls the ball against New York Knicks guard Landry Shamet during a game Sunday, Feb. 1, 2026, in New York. Brad Penner-Imagn Images

It was eventually revealed that Smith was wanted by the Southlake Police Department for assault causing bodily injury — family violence after he allegedly got physical with a woman during a Sept. 18 incident in Tarrant County.

Smith was accused of "intentionally, knowingly, or recklessly" causing "bodily injury" to a female who was a member of Smith's family or household or with whom he had a dating relationship, court documents stated.

Smith was taken into custody over the allegations and released a short time later. His attorney, Justin A. Moore, said in a statement after the run-in with cops that his client was "eager to see this matter resolved and has full confidence in the legal process."

Smith is due in court for his next hearing in the case next month.

#2865. 5 midterm election takeaways: Tech billionaires suffer as Democrats drub Republicans

Democrats decisively took control of Congress Tuesday night, delivering a stinging rebuke to President Donald Trump and his economic record as voters voiced deep frustration with rising prices, government corruption and the AI data center build-out.

The party scored an extraordinary pair of victories in traditionally Republican Texas. James Talarico defeated Ken Paxton, becoming the first Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from the state in nearly four decades. Gina Hinojosa defeated three-term Gov. Greg Abbott, making her the first Democratic governor of Texas since Ann Richards left office in 1995. Texas became the clearest measure of how far the blue wave reached: Democrats swept the state's marquee races in a Republican stronghold long considered beyond their grasp.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The victories were part of a broader wave of Democratic gains. In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews upset Darline Graham in a race Republicans had expected to win. In Ohio, Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted. Democrats also flipped Senate seats in Iowa and North Carolina and won key races in Georgia and New Hampshire.

The results marked a striking collapse for Trump's candidates. Paxton, Graham and Husted were among the president's prominent picks who lost after he made dozens of endorsements.

The election also sharpened the political fight over data centers and artificial intelligence infrastructure. Texas is a major center of planned data-center construction, and Hinojosa campaigned for a pause on new projects until the state adopts tougher rules. Her victory gives Democrats more influence over Texas' approach to the build-out, though the state Legislature remains in Republican hands.

The results also delivered a blow to the tech billionaires and corporate interest groups that poured hundreds of millions of dollars into backing Republican candidates who might be friendly to the massive AI data center projects they hope to build around the country. Elon Musk, the world's richest man and the cycle's largest individual political donor, spent record sums in a failed effort to help Republicans retain power, including in Texas.

Musk's spending may have become a liability as voters raised concerns about government corruption and the growing influence of billionaires in exit polls. Talarico repeatedly attacked Musk on the campaign trail, accusing him of trying to buy "his very own personal United States Senator."

"Tonight's results are a wake-up call for corporate special interests and billionaires who have been rigging the economy," said Rep. Chris Pappas, who defeated former Republican Sen. John Sununu to win an open Senate seat in New Hampshire.

The outcome is poised to reshape the technology sector's relationship with Washington. A more adversarial Congress is likely to intensify scrutiny of tech companies and the AI data center build-out. Markets reacted as the scale of Democratic gains became clear: Dow futures fell and S&P 500 futures slipped Tuesday evening.

Economic frustration was also central to Democratic gains. Many voters blamed Trump's tariffs and the war with Iran for worsening their economic situation, while the cost of living remained a persistent strain.

Democratic control of both chambers gives the party a powerful check on Trump in the final two years of his term, including the ability to block parts of his agenda and intensify congressional oversight. The results sent a clear warning: promises to bring down costs did not overcome voters' frustration with prices, corruption and the country's direction.

Silicon Valley spent record sums trying to shape the election. Instead, it got a Democratic House promising to haul its CEOs before congressional investigators.

Many of the winners ran against not just Trump, but the billionaire donors who helped bankroll Republicans. In North Carolina, Cooper told supporters that North Carolinians "will always matter more than the billionaires and the insiders."

In Texas, Talarico defeated Paxton despite more than $200 million from SpaceX CEO Musk to stop him. On the same ballot, Hinojosa defeated Abbott after blaming him for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying the governor for putting Big Tech interests ahead of local communities.

The GOP had a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans exclusively or nearly so. Crypto, AI, and online gambling interests poured more than $377 million into races.

Not all of Silicon Valley's money ended up on the losing side. LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, one of Democrats' biggest donors, cast the results as "an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies" and took a direct shot at Musk.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote on X late Tuesday. "And while people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

With control of the House comes the power to launch investigations, issue subpoenas and examine the Trump administration and its allies. That's going to be a challenge for tech companies to navigate as they find themselves in Democratic crosshairs.

Democrats are already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the oversight committee, put it more simply: "Lawyer up," he said in a statement.

The results also present headwinds for the infrastructure undergirding AI.

The Texas results appear to be a warning sign for the AI industry in a state projected to overtake Virginia as the world's biggest data center market by 2030, according to JLL. Abbott had long courted data center development before shifting his position this year as the backlash grew. Talarico and Hinojosa both seized on that anger, winning over Republican voters fed up with data centers going up in their communities. Hinojosa has promised to force lawmakers to regulate the industry.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Trump personally pushed states to redistrict their congressional seats mid-decade to cement a Republican majority in the House. The effort largely flopped.

Many of the GOP's redrawn districts ended up falling to Democrats, despite the unprecedented effort to defend or flip them by redrawing boundaries mid-decade.

That includes seats in Texas, Florida and North Carolina, which Democrats won despite Republicans making them safer. In Texas, where Republicans began their redistricting war that then spread to other states, Democrats ended up winning most of the gerrymandered seats.

The redistricting push was always risky because no recent census was available when the maps were drawn.

Trump rolled out the red carpet for Paxton, only for Republicans to lose a Senate seat in Texas for the first time in over three decades.

Talarico's victory in Texas came after Trump endorsed Paxton over the incumbent Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, who held the seat for decades. In endorsing Paxton, Trump said Cornyn had not been sufficiently loyal.

Paxton prevailed in a bitter primary, and Trump's move to tip the scales spurned Senate Republicans, who saw Cornyn as a better and less expensive candidate than Paxton — who had a laundry list of scandals that plagued his campaign.

Sen. Darline Graham, R-S.C., who Trump handpicked to replace her late brother Lindsey Graham, fell to Democrat Annie Andrews. And Republican Michael Whatley lost to former Gov. Roy Cooper in North Carolina.

Democrats linked voter concerns over the cost of living to corruption in government in the lead-up to their colossal victory. The mix of the two helped propel corruption to be a top voter issue rather than a downballot concern.

Public polling indicated before Tuesday night that the issue of corruption was landing with voters. CNBC's All-America Survey conducted in mid-October found corruption was voters' top concern. Exit polls on Tuesday also tied the two issues: 69% said benefits flow to the wealthy in CNBC's Voter Poll.

The issue of corruption has exploded into public view during the second Trump term, as reports of Trump family business deals and the president's own ballooning wealth while in office dominate headlines.

In victory speeches across the country, Democrats made clear they are committed to battling a "rigged" system — a line reminiscent of once-radical Bernie Sanders presidential campaigns.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections. We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top one percent,"  Talarico said. "The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom."

#2864. Broncos' updated practice squad after 5 moves on Tuesday

The Denver Broncos had a busy day on Tuesday, making five changes to the practice squad.

First, Denver signed back Jaret Patterson, defensive back Ugo Amadi and defensive tackle Jordan Miller. The Broncos had room for Patterson after safety Taylor Rapp was promoted to the 53-man roster last week. To make room for Amadi and Miller, Denver released wide receivers Eli Pancol and Cam Ross.

Denver now has a pair of running backs available on the practice squad as Patterson joins Cody Schrader. With RJ Harvey (knee) and Tyler Badie (thumb) both on injured reserve, J.K. Dobbins and Jonah Coleman are the only two running backs currently on the active roster.

Badie will be eligible to return from IR in Week 12 and Harvey will be eligible to return in Week 13. In the meantime, Schrader and Patterson will provide more depth from the practice squad.

Denver's 17-player practice squad and 53-man roster are now full going into Week 9.
