#2863. Player Grades: Knicks 103, Nets 90

For three quarters Monday night, the Knicks looked like they might actually lose to the Nets. The Nets? The last place Nets? While we might get apoplectic, the champs seemed unconcerned about falling behind. When they finally flipped the switch, they turned a 79–75 deficit into a 103–90 win, outscoring Brooklyn 28–11 in the fourth at the Garden. That's 15 straight wins against the neighbors. Landry Shamet did a lot of the work to make it so.

22 points (6–8 FG, 4–6 3PT, 6–6 FT), 1 rebound, 1 assist in 30 minutes

On a night when the starters happily hurled threes like pre-teens playing Pop Shot, Shamet sank a steady four of six, plus every free throw he took. His season-high 22 kept the Knicks in it long enough for the rest of them to wake up.

While the Knicks spun their wheels in third-quarter mud, Josh Hart collected his fourth foul with a lotta game remaining. Shamet replaced him at 8:44 and scored six points on perfect shooting from downtown. The first, at 2:26, cut Brooklyn's lead to a single point; the second, with a minute left, made it 77-75. According to Basketball Reference, he had a team-high game score of 19.4. Pretty good for a sub.

8 points (3–6 FG, 2–5 3PT), 6 rebounds, 7 assists, 3 steals in 27 minutes (+22)

Hart had a crooked finger in just about everything that made the fourth quarter exciting. In that final frame, he hit a Kolek-assisted three to pull the Knicks within one, then assisted on back-to-back Anunoby threes and a Kolek layup (everybody seems to enjoy playing with Tyler, don't they?). Josh intercepted a Julius Randle pass and took it the other way for a layup. His grade suffers for taking just six shots and collecting six fouls, but maybe he deserves a little more love for being a team high +22?

18 points (4–10 FG, 3–7 3PT, 7–8 FT), 4 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 steals in 38 minutes

OG played 38 minutes, got to the line eight times, and hit back-to-back threes in the fourth when the Knicks finally started to put Brooklyn away. Seven free throws will do a lot for a night when only four shots fall from the floor. Give credit to his stamina: after bumping bodies with that bull Julius Randle all night, he still had gas in the tank to help his team in the fourth. He hit the go-ahead three at 10:29 and another bomb 28 seconds later to make it 84–79. He also set up Clarkson's transition three. Loses points for still struggling to finish at the rim.

11 points (4–11 FG, 1–4 3PT), 16 rebounds, 5 assists in 35 minutes

A weird game for Karl-Anthony. He had double-digit rebounds in the first half, but zero points. After intermission, he scraped together 11 points with much more aggressive drives to the rim. As New York rallied in the fourth, he banked in a short fadeaway, grabbed three defensive boards, and found Landry Shamet for the Knicks' final basket. His back up, Andre Drummond, grabbed nine boards in his 13 minutes, but dividing the workload between the pair is unsustainable. James Wiseman is glued to the bench. Leon, what's your move?

19 points (6–16 FG, 4–11 3PT, 3–6 FT), 2 rebounds, 6 assists in 33.5 minutes

Four threes and six assists are dandy, but missing half his free-throws and ten field goal attempts had us a little worried. He's been mostly automatic since coming to New York, so forgive us for jumping to conclusions like: maybe he should spend less time doing extracurricular activities and more in the gym? He started off the game great, making 3-of-4 from deep, but then disappeared for a while.

7 points (3–5 FG, 1–2 3PT) in 11 minutes (+17)

Some guys can do a lot in 11 minutes. Clarkson squeezed seven points into his sliver of time (entirely in the second half) and was still a +17. As the fourth quarter started, a game that had been a nuisance all night was finally starting to break open. Jordan blocked Nolan Traoré's layup and hit a transition three seven seconds later to push the lead to 10. He added a floater at 4:00 to keep the momentum going. He loses a point for collecting two fouls and a technical foul in the span of a minute during the third period, which didn't help matters while the team was flailing.

After the game, coach Mike Brown offered high praise for the vet. "I've been around some good teammates when it comes to players. He's gotta be the best. He's the definition of ultimate pro," he said. "You have a veteran that's been there, done that, and he's willing to accept the role that most guys wouldn't accept […]. And he's willing to accept it… and still try to uplift everybody and connect everybody while he's going through what he's going through. That's the ultimate team player."

6 points (2–4 FG, 2–4 3PT), 1 rebound, 2 assists in 16.5 minutes

Deuce hit two of four from deep and added a couple of assists. A decent shift on a night when the Knicks could've used a few more of those. Two steals is great. Four shots in almost 17 minutes seems insufficient, however.

6 points (1–7 FG, 0–2 3PT, 4–4 FT), 2 rebounds in 16 minutes (−11)

Hoo boy. Bridges made one shot from the floor and did little on defense to make up for it. The Knicks lost his 16 minutes by 11 points, the worst mark in the rotation. At least he got to the foul line for the first time after six games.

It's perhaps too soon to sound the panic alarm, but he has shot 16 of 52 for the season. Mike Brown has taken notice. He ended Kal's night after just a few minutes into the third quarter, and when he sent the starters back at the end of the game, Shamet took Bridges' spot.

The Knicks got away with three quarters of sleepwalking because Shamet kept them close and Hart, OG, and the bench gave them life in the fourth. Next up are the Bulls on Wednesday. In their first meeting of the season, last week, the Knicks won but needed 48 points from the bench to bail them out. Do we see a trend emerging?

#2862. Buyers or Sellers?: A deep dive into potential Commanders trade deadline targets

Once again, Washington finds itself at a familiar crossroads as the NFL trade deadline approaches, trapped in the classic dilemma of whether to buy or sell. However, the internal and external landscape has shifted dramatically. The team's presumed primary trade target, left guard Quenton Nelson was shipped off to Seattle, while their most valuable trade asset, Daron Payne, was lost for the season after suffering a Jones fracture in his foot.

While most front offices in this precarious position would choose to stand pat and do nothing, Washington's circumstances are uniquely complicated. The NFC East remains wide open. Philadelphia is officially tanking, Dallas is imploding and the Giants are an incredibly flawed unit. Because no one else seems to want the division, a playoff berth remains a long, but realistic shot. On the flip side, Washington has glaring roster holes, and the coaching staff hasn't inspired enough confidence to make a true postseason push feel believable. Ultimately, Sunday's matchup against the Rams will likely act as the pendulum that swings the front office in one direction or the other.

In the meantime, a comprehensive blueprint outlines the names to consider selling — and the pieces to look at bringing in.

Dorance Armstrong finally got himself on the sack scoreboard against Philly, but he increasingly looks like the odd man out in this rotation. He is still working his way back into form after an ACL tear derailed a highly impressive start to his campaign last year. While his current market value might not trigger a bidding war, his status on an expiring contract makes him a prime candidate to move. Getting any draft capital in return is better than letting him walk for nothing.

Potential Buyers: Dallas Cowboys, Green Bay Packers, Chicago Bears, Detroit Lions, Cincinnati Bengals

I'm really against trading K'Lavon Chaisson, as he has arguably been the team's most effective pure pass rusher and fits Daronte Jones' defensive scheme to a tee. However, he is also on an one-year deal, which automatically makes him Washington's most valuable healthy trade asset. Contending teams are always desperate for EDGE help down the stretch, meaning Chaisson would command a robust market and bring back the highest return if the front office decides to pivot toward the future.

Potential Buyers: San Francisco 49ers, Green Bay Packers, Chicago Bears, Detroit Lions, Cincinnati Bengals.

While casual observers might scoff at Wylie's trade value; experienced, plug-and-play veteran offensive linemen are incredibly difficult to find midseason. Wylie has built a steady reputation across the league as a stable, high-floor, yet unspectacular right tackle who can also play inside. For a contender dealing with a sudden injury along the front five, Wylie represents an affordable insurance policy who won't derail an offense.

Potential Buyers: Teams requiring immediate, veteran tackle depth or frontline protection stability.

Put the pitchforks down for just a second and hear this out. Everyone knows how vital a high-level backup quarterback is in today's NFL, especially with Jayden Daniels playing a physical brand of football where a single bad hit puts the season in Mariota's hands. However, multiple teams are desperate for immediate quarterback stability and are willing to overpay. If Mariota genuinely wants another shot to start, Washington could leverage that desire into significant draft capital. While a trade remains highly unlikely given his value as a locker room mentor and insurance policy, every player has a price tag if the right offer rolls in.

Potential Buyers: Baltimore Ravens, Minnesota Vikings

These two young defensive backs belong in the same conversation because they present nearly identical dilemmas. If a buyer emerges for either player, it will be a team looking to gamble on their raw traits, hoping a change of scenery can salvage their development and unlock their potential in a different system. At this stage, if a front office offers even an ounce of real draft value for either defender, management should take the deal immediately and run.

Potential Buyers: Buffalo Bills, Pittsburgh Steelers, Chicago Bears, Detroit Lions.

The potential pairing of Jayden Daniels and Jonathan Taylor in the same backfield is nothing short of electrifying. One could easily argue that Taylor represents the most talented, game-changing trade piece currently available on the market. However, that elite pedigree comes with a massive price tag, which might ultimately cause Washington's front office to hesitate. At 27 years old, Taylor carries a heavy career workload on his body. Trading premium assets for a running back with significant mileage always raises red flags. Yet, if he can maintain his current high level of production, he would instantly become the most dominant skill-position player Daniels has ever shared a huddle with in Washington, completely redefining the team's offensive identity.

Value: Low Day 1 or High Day 2 (plus) pick

It seems someone tipped Pollard off about the deadline rumors because he has put on an absolute clinic over the past two weeks. Pollard remains a highly underappreciated player who provides efficiency both as an interior rusher, and a reliable outlet in the passing game. While he may not be a game changer who completely tilts defensive game plans, he is the exact type of high-floor contributor who helps you win tight football games down the stretch. Hitting the age 30 threshold means his price tag is suppressed, but that exact same age factor might sway front offices away from making a move entirely.

Despite recently signing an extension with the Jets, history shows that long-term contracts don't make a player untradeable, look no further than how New York handled Sauce Gardner last year. While Hall is currently sidelined with an injury, his upside remains undeniable. When fully healthy, he is an elite, dynamic weapon out of the backfield, the exact type of premier playmaker that a young quarterback like Jayden Daniels has yet to work with in the NFL. Bringing in an explosive dual-threat like Hall would completely transform the offense's ceiling.

Sitting at a disappointing 0-7, the Chargers are in complete disarray, and their lackluster running game has left a lot to be desired. Ironically, the backfield remains arguably the deepest room on the roster, making it prime real estate for draft capital. Los Angeles needs to accumulate as many assets as possible to jumpstart this rebuild and identify core players who can actually help turn the franchise around.

• Omarion Hampton: As the prize of the group, Hampton possesses the true three-down skillset that will likely command a premium.

• Keaton Mitchell & Kimani Vidal: Both serve as highly intriguing change-of-pace options who could immediately boost a contender's depth chart for a much lower entry cost.

• Projected Value: High-to-Mid Day 3 Pick

I wasn't going to write anything about Downs until I saw some reports about him being tied to the team. Downs has been a solid and reliable WR for the Colts given his size he would be a slot WR wherever he goes and at 25, you're getting a really sound player. I do worry about his next contract. Indy first-hand saw how outrageous the free agent market for WRs can get with the Alec Pierce deal. Investing a ton of money into him while sending out draft capital will the be real question for teams here.

To be entirely frank, a Brian Thomas Jr. departure from Jacksonville still feels highly improbable. Though a strong argument can be made that the Jaguars should trade him, especially given that their current wide receiver depth has made him look like the odd man out in Liam Coen's scheme. Jacksonville remains just one injury away from needing his vertical prowess back on the field. The Jaguars view themselves as legitimate postseason contenders, so retaining premium depth is essential.

Still, from a value standpoint, moving a highly-talented asset who isn't being properly utilized makes complete sense on paper. The logistical hurdle lies in bridging the value gap: Jacksonville will rightfully demand a premium based on his status as a former first-round pick who put up 1,282 yards as a rookie, while rival teams will try to exploit his low 2026 production to buy low. The primary catalyst keeping this hypothetical alive is Thomas' college chemistry with Jayden Daniels at LSU. While navigating the front-office demands will be difficult, if a Day 3 pick is somehow all it takes to reunite the two, Washington should make that call without a second thought.

Of all the wide receiver names circulating on the trade block, Jeudy feels like the safest bet to land in a new home before the deadline. After essentially being phased out and replaced by rookies Denzel Boston and KC Concepcion, Jeudy is clearly the odd man out in Cleveland's long-term plans. The Browns would be incredibly wise to cut ties, move on entirely, and clear out the roster spot.

Ruiz is one of the most intriguing names floating around the trade market. While the Saints are technically still fighting to stay alive in a wide-open NFC South, Ruiz has emerged in league circles as a trade candidate. The logic behind a potential move is two-fold. His play has largely hovered around mediocre throughout his career in New Orleans, and his massive financial footprint is a heavy burden for a cash-strapped Saints franchise. New Orleans did restructure his deal right before the season kicked off, converting base salary to free up short-term cap room. That adjustment loaded his contract with heavy future dead money, which could severely complicate trade logistics. He has struggled to consistently live up to his first-round pedigree but, for a team like Washington, he would still represent an immediate upgrade over Chris Paul at left guard.

The writing is officially on the wall for Jackson Powers-Johnson in Las Vegas. After bouncing all over the interior during his first two seasons, the Raiders benched him as their starting right guard, leaving him displaced and playing completely out of position. Despite his current struggles with consistency, JPJ still possesses immense promise as a true, fundamental center, the exact position he excelled at in college and where his long-term NFL future lies. While the Raiders front office has publicly hinted at retaining their young depth, his sudden appearance on recent injury reports has rival teams wondering if Las Vegas is simply keeping him on ice to protect his health before shipping him out. Given the right environment and an opportunity to slide back over to the pivot, Powers-Johnson is prime for a career redemption arc.

Update: Vegas said they're not interested in trading anyone

Teller is no longer the All-Pro caliber blocker he was during his peak years, but he remains a highly productive player who provides instant stability to the interior of a pocket. The narrative of the Virginia native returning home to finish his career is incredibly clean on paper, and his veteran presence would do wonders for Jayden Daniels' interior protection.

Dickerson represents an absolute walking red flag from a medical standpoint. His history of severe knee injuries suggests his lower body is operating on borrowed time. Even at a discounted rate, trading for this level of medical instability is an unnecessary gamble. The front office should strongly and explicitly advise against executing a move here.

I'll be frank with everyone. There's not a position that frightens me more in terms of future assets than the defensive position. It's bare and next year only Javon Kinlaw, Tim Settle, and Johnny Newton will be under contract. I would like another body in that room. If you don't have a game wrecker, having strong depth in this position is recommended. Barmore has seen his usage dip significantly under Mike Vrabel this season in New England. The Patriots have altered his role to favor third-down pass-rushing packages over early-down run defense, causing his snaps to drop down to 43.6% behind Milton Williams and Cory Durden. While his overall production has tapered off alongside his playtime, Barmore is only 27 years old and has a proven track record of elite interior disruption. Dropping him into a system that asks him to attack more freely could instantly revitalize his career and give Washington a high-floor rotational anchor.

Would Miami move on from 2025 first-round pick if it meant bringing in some more draft capital? That's the real question. Grant remains completely buried on the Dolphins' interior depth chart and was only recently activated after dealing with an injury. Regarded by many draft analysts as a massive reach when he was selected No. 13 overall, Grant has failed to flash any high-end traits over his first two seasons in the league. Crucially, the current front office regime has absolutely zero personal ties to him—they did not draft him, and they owe him no loyalty. If Miami's coaching staff believes this raw project is beyond a rapid redemption arc, they might be highly motivated to sell him off for a reasonable, face-saving package. For Washington, his athletic traits alone make him an ideal low-risk, high-upside flier.

Another name tied to Washington, Hooker has been a rock this year for the Titans. He is fresh off the absolute game of his life, single-handedly terrorizing Joe Burrow by picking him off three times in a single contest. While his stellar production has generated a buzz on the trade market, his current contract structure means acquiring his services shouldn't require a premium king's ransom. For a secondary looking to add high-IQ and reliable playmaking without exhausting foundational draft capital, Hooker represents the perfect targeted upgrade

I do believe Washington's decision will be determined by this must-win game vs the Rams. The front office's blueprint for the next calendar year hangs entirely on this outcome. Win this game, push closer to .500, and we will likely see an aggressive front office willing to part with draft assets to maximize Jayden Daniels' rookie contract window. Conversely, a loss on Sunday could cool those deadline fires, signaling to the front office that the smarter play is to stay patient, hoard their draft equity and address these glaring roster holes through the draft and free agency.

Drop your thoughts in the comments below! Which of these trade targets makes the most sense for Washington? Feel free to add additional names to the board that you think the front office should be calling about before the window locks in!

#2861. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2857. Kevin Durant calls out reporter in uncomfortable exchange: ‘Why does that matter?’

A testy Kevin Durant got into a verbal exchange with a reporter after the Rockets' 109-93 win over the Celtics on Monday night, as the NBA superstar took issue with a question about his limited role in the offense.

Durant had just 12 points on 5-of-6 shooting from the field, while pulling down just three rebounds and recording four assists. When asked about what happened with his limited number of shots, Durant became defensive and the exchange became heated.

"Why does that matter to you? We won the game," Durant said. "Why are you singling me out? [Jabari Smith Jr.] had 30 last game and only had 14 tonight. Are you going to talk about his variety?

Rockets forward Kevin Durant (7) looks up during the third quarter against the Boston Celtics. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

​​Durant didn't let up as the reporter tried to better explain the question.

"What does that have to do with anything?," the Rockets star said. "Are you just watching me all game? Do you have the solo cam? We won the basketball game, bro."

Monday marked the fourth time in the last eight Rockets games that Durant attempted 10 or fewer shots and his 12 points were a season low.

Durant has been historically one of the best scorers in NBA history, sitting fifth on the all-time scoring list.

Eventually, Durant did answer about how his on-court presence affects what his teammates can do.

"My presence on the court, regardless, is opening it up for everybody as soon as I step on the court. They don't have to give me the ball. They're going to double-team. They're going to shade guys to me. Even when I don't have the ball," Durant said. "My presence alone is impactful to the game of basketball. I don't have to shoot. I don't have to dribble. Just me being out there is going to cause a reaction from the defense. My teammates are taking advantage of it."

Rockets' Alperen Sengun, right, blocks Celtics guard Baylor Scheierman, center, as forward Kevin Durant drives. AP Photo/Michael Wyke

Durant is averaging 20.8 points per game this season, and the Rockets are tied with the 76ers for the best record in the NBA at 7-1.

#2856. Bryan Cristante believes Manchester United’s penalty v Roma was too soft

Serie A side Roma fell to another dramatic defeat in the Champions League – this time to Manchester United. The Giallorossi were 2-0 up in the first-half but a Bruno Fernandes penalty and a Mateus Cunha brace helped the Red Devils grab a memorable, comeback victory.

It was also Manchester United's third win in all competitions, with their last one coming against Sabah in the Champions League.

Roma came into the game with a contrasting run of form but found themselves coming across a very familiar fate at Old Trafford.

But controversy surrounded the result and captain Bryan Cristante spoke to the press in the mixed zone about Manchester United's penalty, which came as a result of Gianluca Mancini's suppossed foul on Cunha.

The Italian said – via TMW: "Yes, I didn't see it again, but from the pitch it certainly seemed very, very light to me, especially considering we're playing in Europe, where contact is called much less often. It seemed very light to me. I'll see it again later."

The midfielder also noted that the atmosphere at Old Trafford also helped Manchester United, allowing them to take charge.

"They definitely recharged after the goal and started strong. So that certainly helped recharge the atmosphere."

Even during the game, it was largely clear that Mancini, if he actually did, made very little contact with Cunha. If anything, angles also showed the Brazilian tripping himself over without having been touched by the Italian.

It should ideally be surprising that this hasn't been talked enough. If this incident transpired against the English side, the general reaction in the media would have been far different.

#2855. Giants news: Greg Newsome contract extension, running back rumors, more

Good morning, New York Giants fans! Here are the stories you need to as the Giants get set to face the last place Philadelphia Eagles.

Accountability is vital in Harbaugh's program. This is crucial because it was considered a shortcoming of former coach Brian Daboll and how he led the team. These new Giants are at a point where it's acceptable and common during team meetings for Harbaugh or his assistants to call out individuals for mistakes during practice or games.

"I would say some of [my coaches] have handled it like Harbaugh, some of them haven't. It really just depends on your coaching style and how you want to handle it," veteran offensive lineman Aaron Stinnie said. "But I think guys are taking really good to it and like it. I think it helps. It helps keep everyone accountable and stay on the same page. There's definitely a way to do it and he does a good job of having it in a constructive manner. Just being upfront and honest. Everything is transparency."

With John Harbaugh as the face of the franchise, the shot caller of all football decisions, the New York Giants will never punt opportunities that present themselves.

And this, where they are at the moment, certainly constitutes an opportunity that few if any Giants saw coming.

The first-place Giants won't be sellers, so any trade would likely involve a player who has fallen out of favor. Center John Michael Schmitz lost his starting job to Bryan Hudson in Week 4. Schmitz provides depth at an important position, but a center-needy team could offer a Day 3 pick for the 2023 second-round pick in the final year of his contract.

Safety Tyler Nubin is another recent second-round pick who appears to be on the verge of losing his starting job. Nubin has been a disappointment in New York, but he could be a buy-low option for a team that liked him in the 2024 draft. Defensive tackle Darius Alexander is yet another recent Day 2 pick who could be available as his role has been diminished. The 2025 third-round pick has essentially become unplayable against the run, and he could be made even more expendable if the Giants trade for a defensive tackle.

What comes next is not the most severe football challenge but it is an emotional and psychological hurdle, considering what the rival Eagles have inflicted upon the Giants and their fans for more than a decade.

"Philly is a very raucous atmosphere to play in, the fans are super similar to our fans up here,'' said guard Jon Runyan Jr. "The only difference is they've had something to cheer about the last couple of years recently. I'm hoping the tables turn a little bit. I feel like they have. It's up to us to go down there and take care of business. I think we have what we need. We split with them last year so I think we're building in the right direction.''

When asked about the Giants' 13-game losing streak in Philadelphia dating to 2013, Harbaugh dismissed any notion that the current team inherits that history. He used an analogy about rejecting an unwanted burden.

"I'm trying to think of the analogy because inherit is an interesting word," Harbaugh said. "You're sitting there, you're sitting at one of those, I've only seen them on movies where they all go into the lawyer's office and they read the will, right? What would that be akin to, a 13-game losing streak that you inherit? Yeah, you can say like, no thanks, that's what I would probably say is like. I'd say like, I'm not up for that, so I'm going to say no thanks to that. Yeah, I didn't know that, and I probably didn't need to know that. Yeah, we don't care, we don't care about that."

5. Arvell Reese, LB, Giants: A+. Remember all those concerns about Reese's pro day workout? Seven months later and he's one of the easiest evaluations in the class because he's doing exactly what made him such an exciting prospect at Ohio State. Instincts, range, and insane athleticism, while learning on the job, have all made him an immediate difference-maker, and Giants coaches have praised how quickly he's hit the ground running.

10. Francis Mauigoa, OG, Giants: C+. The move from college tackle to NFL guard hasn't been seamless, but Mauigoa has steadily gotten better. His athleticism and ability to move defenders in the running game stood out at Miami, although he occasionally struggled with balance and hand placement. Those issues have followed him inside, but his improved pass protection, especially vs. Houston in Week 7, was encouraging.

#2854. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2850. Dumont family takes controlling ownership of Mavericks after purchasing shares from Miriam Adelson

Sivan and Patrick Dumont are now the controlling owners of the Dallas Mavericks.

The Dumonts purchased Miriam Adelson's shares of the Mavericks last month, according to ESPN's Tim MacMahon, which made them the controlling owners of the team with a 69% ownership share.

Though she has sold her shares to the Dumonts, Adelson is still very much connected to the Mavericks. Patrick Dumont, who has served as the Mavericks' governor since the families purchased the team from Mark Cuban in 2023, is Adelson's son-in-law. Sivan is Adelson's daughter.

"It's really about long-term investment in the city of Dallas," Dumont told ESPN on Tuesday. "The Dumont family is really committed to building what we hope is the best arena in the NBA, a really significant investment in the city that will help create the new 40-year home for the Dallas Mavericks, a great experience for our fans and inspire our team to win championships. That's really what we're focused on. We had the opportunity, and we're really excited about it."

Cuban sold the Mavericks to Adelson at a $3.5 billion valuation in 2023, though he still maintains a 27% share of the team. He's since admitted that he regretted selling the Mavericks to Adelson and Dumont in the first place, especially after the Dumonts and Adelson pushed him out of the team's basketball and business operations side. Cuban's comments came in the wake of the Luka Doncic trade, which drew intense backlash from Mavericks fans and resulted in an overhaul of the team.

Dumont, who is the Las Vegas Sands CEO, can purchase another 20% share of the team from Cuban in the future at the same valuation, too, something he said he plans to do.

"I just have to tell you, I love Mark. He's been a great family friend for a really long time," Dumont said. "He's just a super passionate Mavs fan and he's frustrated. I respect it. We're going to hopefully do him proud. That's my goal — to make all of our Mavs fans super proud about the success of the team and win a championship."

The Mavericks are expected to open a new arena at the Valley View Mall site in north Dallas in the coming years, and hope to have a new business office and practice facility to go with it. Dumont has overseen construction of the Venetian Arena in Macao, which is home to the NBA's preseason games in the country.

The Mavericks have played at the American Airlines Center since 2001, and hope to move into the new arena in time for the 2031-32 campaign.

"I think what we bring to Dallas is going to be world class," Dumont said. "My goal is to make the best arena in the NBA. It starts with the court and expands outwards, and I want to make sure the fans have the best experience possible, because we owe it to them."

The Mavericks currently hold a 4-3 record entering a matchup with the San Antonio Spurs on Wednesday night. They've not been to the playoffs since their trip to the NBA Finals with Doncic in 2024, and went just 26-56 last season.

#2847. Who needs picks when your offense is lacking?

Every year that passes, I like having draft picks more and more. I fully understand that they are all essentially a roll of the dice, but I'd rather have a bunch of chances at improving the team than not. In general, I'm not normally about trading picks mid-season.

I think this is the perfect time and place to let those picks fly. We just went through the wringer of our schedule where we easily could have had 6 losses at this point instead of 6 wins.

This team is trending up, and the offense needs help. A play-making running back makes way too much sense.

This team legitimately has deep playoff run potential. Shove those chips in the middle on this team this year.

Jonathan Taylor may cost a 2nd, 3rd, and 5th. Breece Hall may be slightly less. Tony Pollard could sneak into the picture for the Broncos.

Honestly, I don't care which one. Any of them will help this team finish the season strong.

#2846. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2842. Rules of Golf Review: In alternate shot, can a player purposely miss so his or her partner can hit?

Playing a competition with a partner can be an uncomfortable experience, especially if one partner is markedly better than the other.

Take the classic game "Pinehurst." Not familiar with it? In that format, each player on a team tees off and then has to play the teammate's ball. From there, you pick the better shot of the two and play alternate shot until the ball is holed. The problem with Pinehurst is that if a low-handicap golfer is paired with a high-handicap golfer, there's a good chance the better player is going to have to hit from spots on the golf course he or she has never been before. It also puts a lot of pressure on the poorer player, as he or she doesn't want to let the better player down by duffing it.

Any time it's a team event, a good bit of the strategy has less to do with the actual swinging of the golf club and more to do with the team's mind-set. Who hits first? What type of shot do you play? Is it better to make the player with the higher score hole out to take pressure off a birdie or par putt? Do you pick up so the other team doesn't get to see the line to the hole?

It can be exhausting having to put so much thought into team strategy. Which brings us to one of the toughest formats in golf: alternate shot (aka foursomes). Teams spend a great deal of time thinking about who tees off on which holes, whether to be aggressive or conservative, and even where to leave shots for the other teammate should a miss occur.

Rules Review Rules of Golf Review: He rotated his golf ball without marking it. He says it's OK because he didn't lift it. True? Ron Kaspriske - Jun 10

Rules Review 6 commonly misunderstood rules you should know Ron Kaspriske - Oct 29

Technology Got a rules question? The USGA is harnessing AI technology to get you fast, accurate answers Ryan Herrington - May 27

Here's a strategy wrinkle you might not have considered. Hypothetically, let's say there's an alternate-shot event pairing a good player with a poorer player. Maybe one handicap has to be south of 15 and the other north. The good player on the team hits a nice approach shot, but it takes a hard hop on the green and rolls through into a deep-rough lie. Making it worse, it's a slick back-to-front sloped green meaning only the most deft touch from this spot will keep the ball from rolling off and into a pond guarding the front.

This is a brutal spot for the higher-handicap player to be in. Then a lightbulb goes off. The player figures that it would be better to intentionally miss the ball than hit it into the water on the other side of the green. It would likely save at least a stroke and keep their side in the hole. What happens when a player in foursomes intentionally misses the ball to give his or her teammate a chance to hit the same shot?

In short, it's still that golfer's turn. A clarification to Rule 22.3 covers this scenario. "A player may not change whose turn it is to play by intentionally missing the ball. A stroke is the forward movement of the club made to strike the ball. Therefore, if a player has intentionally missed the ball, they have not made a stroke and it is still [his or her] turn to play."

If you're wondering what would happen if the player intentionally missed and then his or her teammate hit the shot, the side would get a penalty for playing from the wrong place (losing the hole in match play).

The only time when a whiff would be acceptable in this situation is if the player did not intend to miss the ball, which could easily happen in this situation considering the lie and the potential outcome.

What do I do if my ball just hit another ball on the green?

What happens if my three-minute search for a lost ball gets interrupted?

I hit myself with my own ball. Is that a penalty anymore?

My ball 'might' be in a pond, but I can't find it. Can I drop, add a penalty stroke and move on?

Does a ball have to be unplayable to declare it unplayable?

I accidentally hit my ball with a practice stroke … does it count?

I'm on the green. My opponent is not. Who's away?

My opponent dug his feet in a bunker, got another club and did it again. Is that legal?

I bent my putter and it actually works better. Can I still use it?

#2840. Patriots vs. Dolphins snap counts: O-line shakeup continues

The New England Patriots offensive line has undergone multiple changes since the start of the season, and Sunday's win over the Miami Dolphins saw the latest chapter in the ongoing story being written. After starting Walter Rouse at left guard, the Patriots made a switch at the half to insert Ben Brown into the lineup.

Brown finished the game at the position, and now seems like the frontrunner to remain in that role for the foreseeable future and until starter Alijah Vera-Tucker is cleared to return from injured reserve. Rouse, meanwhile, appears to be headed back to the bench after starting two games in place of Vera-Tucker.

For head coach Mike Vrabel, the change came down to in-game momentum; Rouse had struggled in the first half, prompting the change to a more reliable Brown after two quarters.

"Ben's worked really hard," Vrabel said on Monday. "Coming in, he had worked through the knee injury – would have been Jacksonville, I think. He came back, had been working in practice, and we stuck with Walter and then wanted to take a look at Ben. We'll see where that goes this week. Those guys play extremely hard. It's never going to be perfect. I thought they both did some things well. I thought they both would have some plays that they would like to improve on."

Here is our full snap count report from the Patriots' 24-19 win.

Total snaps: 60 | Time on the field: 31:09 | * denotes starter

QB Drake Maye* (60; 100%), LT Will Campbell* (60; 100%), C Jared Wilson* (60; 100%), RG Matt Pryor* (60; 100%), RT Morgan Moses* (60; 100%), TE Eli Raridon* (55; 92%), WR Efton Chism III* (53; 88%), WR Kyle Williams* (50; 83%), RB Rhamondre Stevenson* (46; 77%), WR DeMario Douglas (39; 65%), LG Ben Brown (34; 57%), LG Walter Rouse* (26; 43%), TE Tanner Arkin* (16; 27%), RB Corey Kiner (11; 18%), TE Hunter Henry (8; 13%), TE Cameron Latu (7; 12%), RB Lan Larison (5; 8%), WR Ryan Miller (5; 8%), OT/TE Caleb Lomu (5; 8%)

Besides the Ben Brown/Walter Rouse combo at left guard, the Patriots offensive line went wire-to-wire on Sunday. That included Matt Pryor, who, while far from perfect either, seems to remain the right guard of choice until one of the higher-ups on the depth chart returns.

At wide receiver, the Patriots opted to leave DeMario Douglas in his role as a third slot receiver even with A.J. Brown on the inactives list. This meant that Efton Chism and Kyle Williams were the main options on the perimeter and in two-receiver sets. All three had their moments, with Douglas and Williams both catching long touchdowns to help New England pull away in the second half.

At tight end, the Patriots got Hunter Henry involved early but he departed with a foot injury in the early second quarter. In his place, rookie Eli Raridon played a career-high 92% of snaps. Tanner Arkin, a regular on the inactives list in recent weeks, also set a new career mark with a 27% playing time share.

Running back continued to be the Rhamondre Stevenson show, with Corey Kiner and recently-promoted Lan Larison only seeing spot duty.

Total snaps: 65 | Time on the field: 26:33 | * denotes starter

LB Robert Spillane* (65; 100%), LB Christian Elliss* (65; 100%), CB Christian Gonzalez* (65; 100%), S Kevin Byard III* (65; 100%), S Craig Woodson* (65; 100%), CB Carlton Davis III* (63; 97%), ED Elijah Ponder* (54; 83%), DT Milton Williams* (44; 68%), ED Dre'Mont Jones* (42; 65%), CB Marcus Jones (40; 62%), DT Christian Barmore* (36; 55%), DT Cory Durden* (26; 40%), DT DaQuan Jones (21; 32%), DT Leonard Taylor III (20; 31%), ED Gabe Jacas (19; 29%), S Jaylen Reed (13; 20%), DT Joshua Farmer (10; 15%), CB Charles Woods (2; 3%)

Coming off a three-game absence due to a neck injury, Carlton Davis returned to the lineup on Sunday and promptly played 63 of 65 defensive snaps — a positive sign even though he did show some rust in man-to-man coverage. The two snaps he wasn't on the field for saw CB4 Charles Woods enter the field. On one of Woods' snaps, he tripped and fell, allowing Miami's Kevin Coleman Jr. to get open for a 35-yard touchdown.

Going against one of the most productive scramble quarterbacks in football, the Patriots opted to increase their use of three-safety packages. This meant that late-August trade acquisition Jaylen Reed saw an uptick in opportunities again, playing 20-plus% of snaps for the first time since Week 5 and fourth time overall this season.

Up front, Gabe Jacas' knee injury in the early third quarter left New England thin on the edge. Elijah Ponder and Dre'Mont Jones, the latter of whom getting dinged up himself at one point, did not see a noticeable uptick in snaps. Instead, New England opted to use their big bodies on the interior to keep the rotation along the defensive line — both inside and outside — intact.

That included Christian Barmore, who has been the recent subject of trade speculation. Against Miami, there was no noteworthy change in his usage. He did return to the starting lineup after a four-game absence, however, and at 55% had his highest playing time share since Week 1. He also clearly out-snapped Cory Durden, who did have the most disruptive game of his career but continued to be used on a rotational basis like the other DTs.

Total snaps: 26 | Time on the field: 2:18

TE Cameron Latu (22; 85%), S Brenden Schooler (21; 81%), LB Darius Muasau (20; 77%), LB Namdi Obiazor (20; 77%), S/PP Mike Brown (20; 77%), LB Erick Hunter (17; 65%), S Jaylen Reed (17; 65%), RB/KR Lan Larison (13; 50%), CB Charles Woods (13; 50%), CB Kindle Vildor (13; 50%), K Andy Borregales (10; 38%), P/H Mitch Wishnowsky (8; 31%), LS Julian Ashby (8; 31%), TE Tanner Arkin (7; 27%), DT Leonard Taylor III (7; 27%), ED Elijah Ponder (7; 27%), OT Caleb Lomu (5; 19%), OT Dametrious Crownover (5; 19%), OL Jared Wilson (5; 19%), OL Matt Pryor (5; 19%), OL Walter Rouse (5; 19%), ED Gabe Jacas (5; 19%), CB/PR Marcus Jones (5; 19%), RB/KR Corey Kiner (4; 15%), DT Milton Williams (4; 15%), DT Cory Durden (4; 15%), DT DaQuan Jones (4; 15%), RB Rhamondre Stevenson (1; 4%), WR Efton Chism III (1; 4%), WR/KR Kyle Williams (1; 4%), WR DeMario Douglas (1; 4%), LB Robert Spillane (1; 4%), LB Christian Elliss (1; 4%), S Kevin Byard III (1; 4%)

The Patriots made some changes to their kicking game operation versus the Dolphins, most prominently in the return game. With Kyle Williams playing a starter-level role on offense, the lead-off duo on kickoff returns consisted of Corey Kiner and Lan Larison. With Miami opting to go for the end zone shot each time, however, neither had a real oppotunity to return kicks on Sunday.

Another change in the kicking game saw defensive tackle Leonard Taylor being used as a part of the kickoff coverage team. At 6-foot-3, 305 pounds he was an unlikely addition to the group, and ended up playing just two snaps. One of them saw him attack too far upfield and contribute to a 43-yard kickoff return in the first quarter.

For a seventh time in eight games this season, Tommy DeVito remained on the bench throughout the game. Unlike the previous contest in Chicago, when he had company in the form of rookies Tanner Arkin and Dametrious Crownover, he was the only player not to take the field against the Dolphins.

QB3 Behren Morton, RB TreVeyon Henderson, WR A.J. Brown, CB Karon Prunty, CB Channing Canada, S Elliott Davison

The Patriots downgraded TreVeyon Henderson (illness) and A.J. Brown (knee) before the trip to Miami, while the other four inactives were all healthy scratches. That included safety Elliott Davison, who has since been released and is expected to rejoin the team via its practice squad. Of course, inactivity does not reflect roster status: cornerbacks Karon Prunty and Channing Canada were both healthy scratches, but New England still opted to cut veteran Kindle Vildor over them on Tuesday.
